# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Security

- **The OCI `/v2` read path now enforces the token's `read:artifacts` action scope** (#3408). `oci_scopes_grant` is the action ceiling for an API token presented directly to `/v2` (as a Bearer token, or in the Basic password field). It was called at seven sites and every one of them asked for `write:artifacts` or `delete:artifacts`; the pull path asked for nothing. `oci_read_permitted` enforced the scan-token pin and the token's *repository* allow-list, then the admin and public-read bypasses, then the `read` permission — but the token's declared *action* ceiling was never consulted for a read. A token minted with `scopes = ["write:artifacts"]` and nothing else could therefore `docker pull` every repository its owner was permitted to read: the push-only CI publish credential was silently also a read credential.

  A pull now takes the same gate the other six verbs take, in the same shape and the same position — immediately after authentication, so it runs **before** the admin bypass, for the same reason the repository allow-list is documented as binding admins: an action ceiling the principal themselves asked for should bind them regardless of `is_admin`. The refusal is `403` with the OCI `DENIED` envelope naming the missing scope, matching the write gate; a `404` would be indistinguishable from a deleted repository and would tell the operator nothing about their token. It applies to every read verb — manifest `GET`/`HEAD`, blob `GET`/`HEAD`, `tags/list`, `referrers`, and `_catalog`. `Claims.scopes` is deliberately untouched: threading the ceiling there would deliver it by demoting `is_admin` through `with_scope_gated_admin`, which roughly 34 raw handler checks also read, changing what the principal *is* everywhere the resulting `AuthExtension` travels (decided on #3322).

  So that the next `/v2` verb cannot re-open the hole, the property is enforced structurally rather than by a test pinned to the sites fixed here. `authenticate_oci` — the wrapper that returned claims and discarded the scope tuple — is deleted, so a handler cannot acquire the identity without also acquiring the ceiling that governs it; and a class-level test scans this module and fails on any production authentication that is not followed by an `oci_scopes_grant` check naming an `OCI_SCOPE_*` constant, unless the call site carries a written `OCI-SCOPE-EXEMPT` justification. This is the same greppable-exemption shape #3399 and #3331 used.

  **Upgrade note.** This is a fail-closed behaviour change. Affected: any API token presented directly to `/v2` whose scope list omits `read:artifacts` and is neither `*` nor `admin` nor the bare parent `read`, being used to pull — concretely, `write:artifacts`-only CI publish tokens that also `docker pull`. Such a pull now returns `403 DENIED` ("Token does not have required scope: read:artifacts"); the fix is to add `read:artifacts` to the token's scopes. Unaffected: unscoped credentials (JWT login sessions, `docker login` with a password, and any token whose ceiling is absent — `oci_scopes_grant` passes `None` through), tokens carrying `*` or `admin`, scanner pull tokens, anonymous pulls of public repositories, and every write or delete path.

- **REST content paths now require the `read` action, not merely any grant, on a private repository** (#3331). `RepositoryService::user_can_access_repo` answered *"does this principal hold any grant on this repository"* and took no action parameter at all, so every one of its call sites got the same any-grant answer — including the ones that return artifact **bytes**. A principal whose only entitlement on a private repository was `{write}` or `{delete}`, or who held a role carrying no `read`, was therefore served that repository's contents through the REST API while the equivalent OCI `/v2` and native-format reads of the same repository correctly refused. The grant predicate tests only `actions <> '{}'`, and `permissions.actions` is `TEXT[]` rather than an enum, so a rule carrying an unrecognised action string qualified too; the `role_assignments` arm never joined `roles`, so a role's own permissions were never consulted.

  `user_can_access_repo` now takes a required `RepoAccess` argument. `RepoAccess::READ` layers the canonical action choke-point — `PermissionService::check_repository_action`, the same function with the same arguments that the direct `/v2` read gate and `repo_visibility_middleware` call — on top of the unchanged tenant gate, so a repository is readable through REST exactly when a direct read of it succeeds, by construction rather than by assertion. All eleven call sites were classified individually. Six now ask for `read`: the artifact-by-id gate and its label/security/SBOM/quality-gate siblings, `/api/v1/tree/content` (which serves bytes), the repo-scoped SBOM and CVE-history reads, the quarantine reason text, and `require_visible` — which alone fans out to roughly 23 read surfaces including `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}`, artifact listings and downloads, storage trees and storage-usage trees, virtual member listings, and the security, signing, curation, approval, promotion-rule, quality-gate and repository-label reads.

  Five sites deliberately keep the tenant gate and say so in the source with a `TENANT-GATE-ONLY` marker: the REST write/delete gate (whose action half is layered separately and canonically by `require_repo_action(.., "write"/"delete", ..)` — naming `read` there would refuse a legitimate publish-only identity), repo-token management (a visibility bar; the delegation ceiling is enforced per-scope at mint time), cross-tenant promotion and the two curation tenant-ownership gates (tenant identity, not a capability, and enforced independently of the admin flag), and repo-scoped webhook management. Making the parameter required means a new call site cannot inherit the action-blind behaviour by omission, and a structural test scans the whole backend source tree and fails on any `RepoAccess::TenantOnly` that does not carry a written justification — the class-level shape #3399 used for virtual member walks, chosen because a test pinned to the sites fixed here would go green while the next content path re-opened the hole.

  **Who is affected.** Only callers reading a **private** repository, and only those whose grant does not carry `read`. Concretely: a fine-grained `permissions` rule with `actions` of `{write}`, `{delete}`, `{write,delete}`, or any value the action vocabulary does not recognise; and a custom role whose `permissions` array omits `read`. The built-in roles all carry it (`admin`, `developer`, `reader` and `repository-owner` each include `read`), so a default installation sees no change. Public repositories, anonymous reads, global admins, and every write or delete path are unaffected. Publish-only automation identities that were reading through these surfaces will now be refused — that is the intended capability removal, and the fix is to add `read` to the grant. This closes the asymmetry #3323 and #3326 left behind, where the virtual-member path was stricter than a direct REST read of the same member.
- **Virtual-repository reads authorize the member set against the caller across every format** (#3323). The format-route visibility middleware authorizes only the repository named in the URL — the virtual PARENT — while everything a virtual repository aggregates is a separate repository with its own ACL, so an anonymous request to a **public** virtual over **private** members reached each member's content unchecked. The filter existed (`proxy_helpers::authorize_virtual_members`) but was applied on a minority of call sites, and the two shared metadata primitives (`resolve_virtual_metadata`, `collect_virtual_metadata`) took no caller at all, so every one of their callers inherited an unfiltered walk. All content-serving virtual read paths — PyPI simple index (root and per-project), npm packument and version metadata, cargo sparse index, Helm `index.yaml`, NuGet V2/V3 search, registration and version indexes, Conda repodata/channeldata, CRAN `PACKAGES`, Hex names/versions/package, RubyGems spec indexes and quick gemspecs, Conan search/revisions/package listings, Composer `packages.json` and p1/p2 metadata, Pub package and version info, Swift release listing and metadata, Go `@v/list` and `@v/*.info`, Debian `Release`/`InRelease`/`Packages`, Alpine `APKINDEX`, Maven root listing, and the Hugging Face `ETag`/`X-Repo-Commit` resolution — now resolve members through one shared helper, `proxy_helpers::authorized_virtual_members`, which is `fetch` composed with the same caller predicate a direct read of that member would apply. The two primitives now REQUIRE a caller, so their remaining callers cannot regress silently, and a structural test scans every format handler and fails on any raw member walk that neither authorizes nor carries an explicit `UNFILTERED-ENFORCEMENT` marker. Enforcement walks — the OCI scan-verdict deny-set, the PEP 708 isolation and shadowing guards, priority maps, cache invalidation and cycle detection — are deliberately left unfiltered, because narrowing a deny-set or a shadowing decision by caller visibility would let a caller who cannot see a member escape that member's gate.

  Three caller-INDEPENDENT caches sat in front of documents that are now caller-dependent, which would have moved the leak rather than closed it: the npm computed-packument cache, the cargo in-process sparse-index cache, and the Maven virtual `maven-metadata.xml` merge cache. Each is now used only when the aggregated document cannot vary by caller — that is, when every member is public, the exact condition under which the authorized member set is identical for anonymous and authenticated callers. A virtual repository with a private member computes per request; all-public virtuals keep their cache.

  Not included: the RPM `repodata` walk (`repomd.xml` and friends). Its document is rendered once and cached per virtual repository id, and `repomd.xml.asc` is a detached signature over the rendered bytes, so a per-caller document breaks both the shared cache and the reproducible-signature invariant (#2636). It carries an explicit `UNFILTERED-DEFERRED` marker in the source and remains tracked on #3323 pending a cache re-keying design.
- Unrecognised scanner severity tokens now classify as `high` instead of `info`, so ungraded findings fail closed at severity gates (#3306). This applies to every scanner adapter (Trivy/Grype/Harbor image reports, filesystem scans, OpenSCAP), covering tokens such as Trivy's `UNKNOWN`, XCCDF's `unknown`, and any vendor-specific grade the classifier does not recognise. Explicitly graded tokens are unchanged, `negligible` (a recognised distro triage grade) stays at `info`, and the OSV/GHSA advisory-ingestion fallback remains independently at `medium`. Note this is deliberately stronger than the `medium` originally proposed on #3306: a `max_severity='high'` policy blocks only `critical` and `high`, so a `medium`-bucketed ungraded finding would still have been served under the default posture.
- **Alpine/apk repository signatures are now verifiable by real `apk`** (#3021). A signed Alpine repository was rejected by `apk` with `BAD signature`, so operators had to either fail closed or fall back to `--allow-untrusted` — a signature the ecosystem client cannot check is a false assurance, not a protection. Three parts of the `.SIGN.RSA` contract were wrong at once: the signature was computed over the *uncompressed* `APKINDEX` text (apk digests the *compressed* index segment as served), with a SHA-256 digest (apk fixes SHA-1 for a `.SIGN.RSA.` entry; `RSA256`/`RSA512` select the others), and the result was appended as an extra member of the index tar (apk expects a separate, "cut" leading gzip stream, the layout `abuild-sign` produces). A served `APKINDEX.tar.gz` is now the concatenation of a signature gzip stream — one `.SIGN.RSA.artifact-keeper.rsa.pub` tar entry, no end-of-archive blocks — and the index gzip stream it signs, byte-for-byte as signed. Verified end-to-end against apk-tools 2.14: `apk update` and `apk add` succeed against a signed repository with `artifact-keeper.rsa.pub` in `/etc/apk/keys/`, while a tampered index and an untrusted key are both refused. Unsigned repositories are unaffected, remote/virtual repositories still proxy upstream indexes untouched, and a signing failure still fails closed with a 500 rather than serving an unsigned index (#2660).
- **Cookie-authenticated web-UI mutations must carry the `X-Requested-With` header** (#3065). The web UI authenticates with an httpOnly session cookie and had no CSRF defense-in-depth behind the cookie's `SameSite=Strict` attribute. State-changing requests (`POST`/`PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE`) that are authenticated by the session cookie *and* are browser-originated are now refused with `403` unless they carry the custom header the web UI sends on every request — a header a cross-site HTML form cannot set at all, and that a cross-origin `fetch()` cannot set without a preflight this server does not approve. The browser-origination test reuses the existing Fetch-Metadata/`Accept` detector added in #3082 rather than adding a second one. The `SameSite=Strict` half of the contract, which remains the primary mitigation, is now pinned by a test that scans every backend source file for session-cookie literals, so a new `Set-Cookie` call site cannot silently omit it.

  **Upgrade note:** this is a behavior change for any non-browser client that authenticates to `/api/v1` with the `ak_access_token` **cookie** and issues state-changing requests while also looking browser-like (sending `Sec-Fetch-*` or an `Accept` listing `text/html`) — such a client must now send `X-Requested-With` (any value) or, preferably, switch to a Bearer/API token. Native package-manager clients (`pip`, `npm`, `cargo`, `docker`, `maven`, …) and every token- or Basic-authenticated API call are explicitly exempt and unchanged: the requirement is keyed on the credential actually being the cookie, matching the middleware's existing credential precedence.

### Added

- **Ansible Galaxy client-contract integration tests and an operator docs page** (#3283). The Ansible format had no file in `backend/tests/` at all, and the conformance scripts that do exist assert on HTTP status codes only. Between them, three client-visible breaks shipped behind green CI: discovery registered at `/api/` while `ansible-galaxy` requests `/api` (#3137), `Authorization: Token <key>` rejected as a malformed header (#3137), and a publish response missing the `task` key the CLI indexes unconditionally (#3282). None of them is visible to a test that picks its own URL spelling, its own auth scheme, and reads only a status code.

  `backend/tests/ansible_galaxy_tests.rs` deliberately inverts each of those blind spots. It drives the **production composition** — the real handler table nested at `/ansible` behind `repo_visibility_middleware`, which is where the reported `401` came from and which the handler's own unit tests never reach — against a **private** repository, so the middleware is load-bearing rather than waved through. It authenticates with `Authorization: Token <api_key>` exclusively, the byte-for-byte header ansible-core emits (`GalaxyToken.token_type = 'Token'`), including on the `auth_required=False` discovery probe that `_add_auth_token` attaches it to anyway. It navigates the install path by **following the hrefs the server advertises** rather than constructing URLs, so a wrong advertised URL fails the test instead of being quietly bypassed, and it round-trips the advertised `download_url` back to the published bytes. It re-implements ansible-core's own `_urljoin` and its `task`-to-poll-URL derivation (keep the last non-empty path segment, rebuild from `api_server`) so the publish contract is checked the way the client checks it, through `state`, `finished_at` and `messages`. `list_collections`, `version_list` and `version_info` had zero coverage of any kind before this and are now covered, as are the `403` on a token missing `write:artifacts` and the rule that a rejected upload carries no `task`. Registered in the CI integration-test job.

  Also adds `docs/ansible-galaxy.md`: `ansible.cfg` / `--server` / `ANSIBLE_GALAXY_SERVER_*` configuration against an Artifact Keeper repository, how to mint the API key the `Token` scheme carries and which scopes it needs, the publish/import-task contract, an endpoint reference, and a troubleshooting table. Note the Starlight documentation site now lives in `artifact-keeper-site`; this page should be mirrored there.

- **`docs/sso-saml.md`: how to find and formulate the SAML ACS URL** (#2583, partial). The ACS URL embeds `saml_configs.id`, a server-generated UUID, and there was no documentation for obtaining it — operators were reading it out of Postgres or scripting Terraform `null_resource` API calls to recover it. The new page documents the URL shape, three ways to obtain the id (the unauthenticated `/api/v1/auth/sso/providers` list, the admin `GET /api/v1/admin/sso/saml` list keyed by the already-`UNIQUE` provider `name`, and capturing it from the creation response), what `use_absolute_acs_url` changes about the `Destination`/`Recipient` binding, and the workarounds for a rebuilt environment. The requested *configurable* identifier is not implemented here: the analysis on #2583 explains why the safer shape is an additive, validated `slug` accepted alongside the UUID rather than a primary-key change, which would rewrite the ACS URL of every deployment that currently works.
- **Per-repository outbound proxy configuration** (#2469, #2811, migration 205). A remote repository can now route its upstream traffic through a specific egress proxy, so a segmented-network deployment can send different upstreams out via different network paths. Configured at `PUT`/`GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/egress-proxy`, which — like `upstream-auth` (#2603) — requires repository `admin`, not merely `write`.

  **Precedence, stated explicitly because an egress control whose effective value is ambiguous is a bug waiting to happen.** A per-repository setting **overrides** the process-wide `HTTP_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY`/`NO_PROXY` environment; it never composes with it. Three modes:

  - `inherit` — follow the process proxy environment. The default, and the state of every repository that predates this change, so behaviour is unchanged unless an operator opts a repository in.
  - `direct` — never proxy this repository, even when the environment sets a proxy. This is the other half of #2811 ("certain repos require a proxy, others do not") for a deployment whose global posture is proxied.
  - `explicit` — use this repository's own `proxy_url` and `no_proxy` list, ignoring the environment entirely.

  Merging the per-repo and global settings was rejected deliberately: composing two proxy URLs and two `no_proxy` lists has no unambiguous meaning. A stored `explicit` config whose URL is missing resolves to `direct`, not `inherit`, so a half-written config fails closed instead of silently falling back to the ambient path.

  **Credentials.** A proxy URL may embed `user:pass@`, so it is treated as credential material on the same path the upstream credentials already use rather than a second secret-handling scheme: encrypted at rest in `repository_config` with the same hex-wrapped AES-256-GCM envelope as `upstream_auth_credentials`, never returned by the API in raw form (the read-back is redacted to `http://***@proxy:3128`, plus a `proxy_credentials_configured` boolean, mirroring `upstream_auth_configured`), never logged (`EgressProxyConfig` has a hand-written redacting `Debug`), and never placed inside the URL string handed to `reqwest` — the userinfo is split off and applied via `Proxy::basic_auth` so the proxy object's own rendering carries nothing either. `proxy_url` / `egress_proxy_url` are also added to the audit-detail redaction key list. Switching a repository away from `explicit` deletes the stored ciphertext rather than orphaning it.

  **SSRF.** A per-repo proxy is attacker-adjacent outbound routing, so three things hold. (1) The proxy URL is validated in a new `TrustedInternal` trust class: RFC1918/CGNAT/IPv6-ULA are permitted, because a corporate egress proxy legitimately lives on the internal network and requiring an allowlist entry would make the feature unusable in exactly the deployments it exists for — but cloud-metadata, loopback, link-local and unspecified addresses stay hard-blocked, so the proxy field cannot be repurposed into a metadata reader or a localhost port-scan oracle. (2) The destination keeps its existing upstream-context guard; a configured proxy never relaxes it. (3) Most importantly, routing through a proxy means `reqwest` does not resolve the destination locally at all — it dials the proxy and hands over the hostname — so the connect-time SSRF DNS guard would silently stop covering that repository. The proxied path therefore re-applies the destination check explicitly before issuing the request. That check is resolution-at-check-time, so it cannot close DNS rebinding for proxied fetches the way the resolver does for direct ones (we are not the party doing the final resolution); this is recorded alongside the pre-existing residual-gap note in `api::validation`. The proxy's own address is exempted from the DNS guard by exact full-host equality only, extending the existing #2570 mechanism to per-repo proxies without widening it for any other target.

  All outbound paths for a proxied repository go through its client — buffered and streaming fetches, conditional `If-None-Match` revalidation, the OCI bearer token exchange and its retry — so the routing cannot be escaped via the auth flow. Clients are cached per repository (60 s TTL, mirroring the custom-User-Agent cache) and invalidated in-process on write so a change applies immediately.

  Two existing weaknesses surfaced while implementing this and are fixed here. `log_proxy_env()` printed `HTTP_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY`/`ALL_PROXY` verbatim at INFO on every client build, including any embedded `user:pass@`; it is now redacted the same way. And AWS's IPv6 instance-metadata address `fd00:ec2::254` sits inside the unique-local range `fc00::/7`, which the `TrustedInternal` class permits unconditionally — so an operator-configured endpoint could be pointed at IMDS over IPv6 even though the IPv4 form was refused. IPv6 cloud-metadata addresses now have their own hard block in `api::validation`, applying to every context, which closes the same gap for the scanner-adapter URL (#2389) as well.
- **Proxy-cached artifacts are now visible to the security surface, and have an SBOM** (#3394, #3395, #3396, migration 202). Proxy-cached content has been scanned at download time and blocked on policy violation since #2954, but none of it was readable: proxy bytes deliberately have no `artifacts` row (#1278/#1280), so every artifact-keyed security read — CVE history, SBOM, security score — is structurally empty for them.

  - `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/security/proxy-scans` — verdict summary and cached-path listing, carrying the repository's `scan_on_proxy` / `proxy_scan_action` enforcement context so `vulnerable` is not ambiguous between a repository that blocks the pull and one that serves it anyway.
  - `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/security/proxy-sbom` — CycloneDX or SPDX, generated on demand from the recorded component inventory. Proxy SBOMs are deliberately **not** stored in `sbom_documents`: that table's `artifact_id` is `NOT NULL` with an FK to `artifacts`, so making it nullable would touch every SBOM query in the codebase and put a cross-tenant `ON DELETE CASCADE` in the path. No existing SBOM handler or query was modified.
  - **Per-CVE detail for proxy content** (#3395). `proxy_scan_results` stored counts and a max severity but not *which* CVEs were found, so a blocked build could be told "9 findings, 4 high" and still not learn which CVE caused it — identifying them required re-scanning the cached bytes by hand, outside the product. The findings already existed in memory at `aggregate_proxy_verdict` and were discarded once the counts were computed; they are now persisted to a digest-keyed `proxy_scan_findings` table and returned on a single-path (`?path=`) read of `/security/proxy-scans`. Written alongside the verdict but independently fallible, so a detail-write failure can never prevent a vulnerable artifact being recorded as vulnerable, and deduplicated on the way in for the same pin-duplication reason that inflated `findings_count`.
  - **On-demand rescan** (#3396). `POST /api/v1/repositories/{key}/security/proxy-scans/rescan` scans a path already in the cache — `proxy_cache_artifacts.storage_key` is `NOT NULL`, so the bytes are read straight from object storage with no upstream fetch and no dependency on the artifact being requested again. This is the remedy for anything cached before inventory recording shipped, which otherwise had no SBOM and no way for an operator to ask for one. It records through the same `proxy_scan_and_record` write path the inline gate uses, so a rescanned verdict and a pulled verdict cannot disagree; it requires repository **write** access and is throttled to one rescan per repository per 30s (`429` with `Retry-After`) because it converts one request into a full scanner run.

  All of these are **path-keyed, never digest-keyed**. Verdicts and inventories are global by content digest, so accepting a digest parameter would turn them into a cross-tenant lookup oracle; a path is inherently scoped to the calling repository. All authenticate unconditionally, including on public repositories, where `require_visible` would otherwise return early.
- **2FA enforcement policy: require TOTP enrollment for admins, or for everyone** (#2805, migration 198). TOTP was fully implemented per user but strictly opt-in, with no way for an operator to require it. A new system-wide policy — `disabled` (the default, byte-for-byte the historical behaviour), `required_for_admins`, `required_for_all` — is read and written at `GET`/`PUT /api/v1/admin/settings/totp-policy` and can be pinned by the `TOTP_POLICY` environment variable.

  **Chosen semantics, stated explicitly because this changes login behaviour when enabled:**

  - **Scope.** The policy is evaluated only on the interactive local password login (`POST /api/v1/auth/login`). API tokens, service accounts, package-client basic auth, already-issued sessions and the refresh grant are untouched, so enabling it never breaks CI, `docker pull`, or a running `pip install`.
  - **Exemptions.** SSO/LDAP/CI-authenticated users are exempt — their MFA is delegated to the identity provider and Artifact Keeper holds no password for them. Service accounts are exempt because they cannot type a code.
  - **Grace = "finish enrolling inside this login", not a countdown.** A covered user who has not enrolled is never rejected. Instead of a session, the login returns `totp_enrollment_required: true` plus a 10-minute, single-use, type-scoped *enrollment ticket*. The ticket authorizes exactly two new public endpoints — `POST /api/v1/auth/totp/enroll/setup` (repeatable: returns the secret and `otpauth://` URL) and `POST /api/v1/auth/totp/enroll/complete` (single-use: verifies the first code and returns the real session **and** the backup codes in the same response). There is no state in which a valid password yields no way in.
  - **Lockout safety, guard 1.** `PUT` refuses to *tighten* the policy unless the administrator making the change already has TOTP enabled — a "you first" rule. That administrator's sign-in path (password + `/auth/totp/verify`) is the pre-existing, already-working one, so at least one administrator provably retains access regardless of how any particular client handles the new enrollment exchange. Loosening the policy is **never** refused, in either direction, so an operator cannot be trapped in the strict state.
  - **Lockout safety, guard 2 (offline break-glass).** `TOTP_POLICY=disabled` plus a restart overrides the stored setting and needs no working login at all. While the environment variable is set the policy is pinned and `PUT` returns `409` explaining how to unpin it. An unparseable `TOTP_POLICY` is ignored with a warning rather than defaulting either way.
  - **A policy change does not invalidate anything.** Existing sessions and refresh tokens survive; enforcement begins at each user's next interactive login, so the enabling administrator keeps their session. Completing forced enrollment *does* revoke that user's prior sessions and refresh-token families, since enrollment is a credential-posture change.
  - **`POST /auth/totp/disable` returns `409` while the policy covers the caller** — enforcement that only bit at the next login would not be enforcement. This adds no lockout path: disabling already required a valid current code, so anyone who can reach it can still sign in.
  - `TOTP_ENROLLMENT_REQUIRED` (classified as a `denied` outcome — the password was right but no session was issued) and `TOTP_POLICY_CHANGED` audit actions are recorded. `GET` also reports how many local administrators/users are enrolled, so the blast radius of a change is visible before making it.

  Backend only. The web UI lives in `artifact-keeper-web`; until it handles `totp_enrollment_required`, an operator on `required_*` should complete enrollment through the API (or use the `TOTP_POLICY` break-glass). The "you first" guard exists precisely so that gap cannot lock everyone out.

- **`auth.admin_break_glass_enabled` in `GET /api/v1/system/config`** (#2571). The public system-config endpoint now advertises whether the verified-admin break-glass local login is available, mirroring `local_login_gate` for an admin caller: `true` unless an SSO provider is enabled *and* the operator opted into strict SSO-only via `SSO_DISABLE_ADMIN_BREAK_GLASS` (#2018). This is the backend flag the login UI needs to render a discoverable "Sign in with admin" button next to the SSO buttons instead of hiding break-glass behind the undocumented `?fallback=local` parameter. Display-only: the login endpoint keeps enforcing the same policy server-side, so the flag never widens access. The existing `local_login_enabled` flag is unchanged and still only advertises the full local form on explicit `ALLOW_LOCAL_ADMIN_LOGIN=true` opt-in.

### Documentation

- **The supported rollback procedure is documented in the operations docs, not only in a changelog entry** (#3314). `SKIP_MIGRATIONS=true` is how you roll a deployment back to an earlier release, and the recipe previously lived only in the 1.7.3 upgrade note — which scrolls out of view at the next release. [`docs/operations/rollback.md`](docs/operations/rollback.md) now covers it permanently: why an older binary refuses to start (a `_sqlx_migrations` **ledger** check that runs before any data is read, not a data incompatibility — operators who misread it as the latter hesitate to proceed, and the hesitation is the expensive part), why the flag is safe rather than merely a silenced safety check (it takes a branch that never constructs the `Migrator`, and migrations are additive so the older binary keeps writing correct rows), why deleting rows from `_sqlx_migrations` is the wrong alternative, the boundary condition where the recipe stops holding (a migration that drops or renames a column the older binary reads, which the flag cannot help with — plus the `git diff` that checks for one, since nothing today marks a migration destructive), and how far back it is actually verified (the 1.7.3 → 1.7.1 pair; anything wider is labelled untested rather than implied). `ARCHITECTURE.md` links to it from the Database section.

### Fixed

- **`docker-publish` no longer cancels its own in-flight image builds on a burst of merges to main** (#2099). The workflow cancelled superseded branch pushes to "stop building stale containers". Its arm64 legs run on GitHub-hosted `ubuntu-24.04-arm` and finish fast, but its amd64 legs run on the capped self-hosted `ak-ci-runners` pool and were still in flight when the next merge landed — so the cancel killed them mid-build and the multi-arch **manifest** jobs, which fan in from both arches and are the only jobs that move the `:dev`/`:main` floating tags, never ran at all. After merging #2092, #2090 and #1927 back-to-back on 2026-07-01, `artifact-keeper-scanner-adapter:dev` was simply never published and iac PR #186's `Verify Image References` gate had nothing to resolve. `cancel-in-progress` is now the literal `false`: runs queue and every one of them finishes. This workflow *publishes*, so a cancelled run is not a re-runnable pass/fail verdict, it is a registry left pointing at a stale digest — the intermediate runs cost build minutes but leave the registry consistent at every step, whereas cancelling saved those minutes by gambling that the next run completes, and when merges arrive faster than the amd64 build takes, that gamble loses every time. Release **version** tags were never affected (tag pushes were already excluded). A regression guard in `backend/src/config.rs` scans **every** `.github/workflows/*.yml`, classifies any workflow that pushes container images (`push: true`, `docker push`, `imagetools create`, ignoring comments), and fails if one declares a top-level `cancel-in-progress` that is not the literal `false` — an expression is explicitly not good enough, since the one this replaced evaluated to true on exactly the branch pushes that publish the floating tags.
- **Flaky NuGet `_db_tests` failing with `503` under full-suite parallelism** (#3407). `api::handlers::nuget::{push_db_tests, read_db_tests}` failed intermittently with `503 Service Unavailable` on the request itself rather than on any assertion — up to ten together on a bad run, clean on others, on branches touching no NuGet code.

  **The reported diagnosis was wrong, and the correction is the fix.** #3407 attributed the shed to the bcrypt auth-concurrency semaphore. Running the full suite against a real database shows zero bcrypt sheds; every failure carries `Too many concurrent uploads for this repository; please retry shortly`, which is the **ingest-extraction** budget in `util/bounded_archive.rs`, an entirely different limiter.

  That budget has two fast-fail (never queueing) caps: a global one defaulting to 8 concurrent archive decodes, and a per-tenant sub-limit of 4. The sub-limit is keyed on an ambient `TenantKey` installed by `run_with_tenant_scope`, which `repo_visibility_middleware` sets once the repository resolves. The handler tests drive routers built by `tdh::Fixture::router_with_auth`, which does not include that middleware — so `current_tenant()` returns `TenantKey::Unattributed` for **every** test in the binary, and all ~15k of them contend for a single four-permit bucket. The fifth simultaneous decode anywhere in the suite sheds, immediately, with no queue. Both caps are now sized for the harness under `cfg(test)` (never compiled into the shipped binary; `backend/tests/` links the library without `cfg(test)` and keeps production values). Production defaults, env overrides and the fairness behaviour are untouched — only the number of permits the test binary hands itself changes.

  Also landed from the original diagnosis, on its own merits: the crate now hashes at bcrypt cost 4 under `cfg(test)`. It was not the cause of this flake, but `hash_password` holds an auth permit for ~100-300 ms at `DEFAULT_COST` and the fixtures throughout the crate already hash at cost 4, so paying a production work factor in a test binary was pure waste. The constant-time rejection pad (`dummy_bcrypt_hash`) now tracks the same cost factor instead of being pinned at 12, because bcrypt reads its work factor out of the hash being verified: a pad at a *different* cost from real hashes would make the "token prefix not found" path take visibly different time from the "wrong secret" path, reintroducing exactly the timing oracle the pad exists to close. Separately, the in-crate fixtures each hardcoded `auth_max_concurrency: 8`, and because that semaphore installs through a `OnceLock` the effective cap was whichever fixture built an `AppState` first; they now share one constant sized for the harness.

  Deliberately **not** fixed with a serial lock. That is the correct fix for #3402 (genuinely shared database state) and would make this worse by holding permits longer while serialised tests queue behind them. The two share only the symptom.
- **Federated logins no longer invent a broken placeholder email address** (#3416). `users.email` is `VARCHAR(255) UNIQUE NOT NULL`, but every federated protocol treats the address as optional, and three login paths had never been migrated onto the shared derivation #3161 introduced for the OIDC and SAML *service* paths.

  The consequential one is the **DB-configured OIDC provider callback** (`handlers/sso.rs`) — a second, independent OIDC implementation that the #3161 fix never touched. It read a missing `email` claim as `.unwrap_or_default()`, collapsing "absent" to a shared `""`. Because the column is `UNIQUE`, the *first* user whose IdP omits the claim provisioned fine with `email = ''` and the **second** failed on `users_email_key` — so a login broke later, for a different user, with an error that named neither the real cause nor the account that caused it, and the row holding `''` stayed put, so it never self-healed. That is bug #3119 verbatim, in a path nobody had noticed existed.

  The two LDAP sites (`extract_user_from_entry`, and the bind-identity fallback taken when the directory search fails) derived `{username}@unknown`. That was keyed on the *pre-uniquification* username, so two directory entries that are distinct identities but normalize to the same username derived the same address and collided on the same constraint; and `.unknown` is not a reserved TLD, so a synthetic address could in principle resolve rather than being inert by construction. Both now key on the entry's **DN** — the value `get_or_create_user` already stores in `users.external_id` and looks the account up by, which makes the synthetic address exactly as unique and as stable as the account row itself — under a new RFC 2606 `no-email.ldap.invalid` domain alongside the existing OIDC and SAML ones.

  All three now call `services::federated_email::resolve_federated_email`, so a fourth variant cannot drift. No privilege boundary is involved: federated identity matching keys on `external_id` + `auth_provider` in every provider path, never on email, so a colliding placeholder could not resolve one user as another — the `UNIQUE` constraint turned it into a hard provisioning failure instead. The impact was availability and diagnosability. Two existing LDAP tests asserted the old `@unknown` values and have been updated deliberately. Users already provisioned with a placeholder address keep it; nothing is rewritten on upgrade.
- **CVE blast radius no longer answers zero for proxy-cached content** (#3397). `GET /api/v1/admin/security/cve/{cve_id}/blast-radius` joins `download_statistics` and `artifacts`, both keyed on `artifacts.id`. Proxy-cached content has no `artifacts` row by design (#1278/#1280) and its pulls land in `proxy_download_statistics` keyed by `proxy_cache_id`, so for a CVE present only in proxied content the report did not merely undercount — it was structurally incapable of returning anything but zero, and a zero to "who is affected by this CVE?" reads as "nobody". The response now carries a `proxy_exposure` block (affected digests, repositories, downloads, distinct downloaders, anonymous flag) computed over the new `proxy_scan_findings` table joined to the proxy cache catalog and `proxy_download_statistics`. It is present even when every field is zero, with an explicit `coverage` of `included`, so "searched, nothing found" is distinguishable from "not searched"; an artifact-id target reports `not_applicable`, since an artifact id can never name proxy content. The proxy numbers are reported alongside the hosted summary rather than folded into it — the two have no join key in common, and merging them would make `affected_artifact_count` mean two different things at once. The download window is applied to the `LEFT JOIN` rather than to a `WHERE`, so a windowed query still reports digests that are cached but were not pulled inside the window.
- **Proxy-cached artifacts are no longer told they cannot be security scanned** (#3344). `ARTIFACT_NOT_ANALYZABLE_MSG` claimed "SBOM generation and security scanning are available only for artifacts hosted in this registry". The SBOM half was accurate; the scanning half had been false since #2954, which built a parallel digest-keyed scan pipeline (`proxy_scan_results`, enforced at serve time by `proxy_helpers::gate_proxy_scan_serve`) without touching `sbom.rs`, so nothing pointed at the string. Acting on the message would have led an operator to mirror everything into hosted repositories to obtain coverage they already had. The single blanket constant is replaced by one message per capability — SBOM generation, on-demand scanning, signing, CVE history — each stating only its own limitation, naming the formats download-time proxy scanning is actually wired for (PyPI, npm, Docker/OCI, not Maven/Go/NuGet/RubyGems), and pointing at the endpoint that answers the question for proxied content. Signing deliberately still dead-ends: there is no proxy signing equivalent, and inventing a pointer would be the same class of stale claim. `signing.rs` carried the same phrasing and is corrected too.
- **Proxy scan `findings_count` is no longer doubled for PyPI wheels** (#3394). `ScanWorkspace::prepare_pinned` catalogs a wheel's component twice — its own `METADATA` plus the synthetic pin written into the workspace root — so the scanner reported every CVE against both copies and the recorded count was exactly double the true one. Findings are now deduplicated on `(vulnerability, component)` before the counts and severity buckets are computed. Relatedly, cached upstream index responses (HTML/JSON directory listings, which have no package inventory) are excluded from proxy artifact listings and scan counts; they were previously counted as `not_scanned` with reason `unknown`, a permanent false positive no operator action could clear.
- **Large artifact uploads no longer fail once they take longer than the global request timeout** (#3263). `GLOBAL_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` (120s by default) was applied as a `tower` timeout over the *whole* router, and that clock covers the time the client spends streaming the request body — so on upload and download routes a duration cap silently became a **size** cap. A ~90 MB `PUT /api/v1/repositories/{repo}/artifacts/{path}` over a typical WAN link exceeds 120s and was aborted mid-body; because the error response is emitted while the client is still writing, the connection is reset and the client never reads the status at all (`curl: (56) Recv failure: Connection was reset`, `HTTP_STATUS:000`). Raising `MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE` could not help: the limit being hit was time, not bytes. Artifact **byte-transfer** routes — repo-scoped `/artifacts` and `/download` paths, the resumable `/api/v1/uploads` session API, OCI blob/manifest transfers, and the Git LFS / incus / lxc protocols whose routers already declare multi-GB bodies — are now exempt from that timeout. Upload size is still bounded by `MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE` (enforced mid-stream), and the load-shed + `GLOBAL_MAX_CONCURRENCY` backstop still bounds how many transfers run at once, so the "no request path can pin every worker" protection is preserved for the server-side work it was written for; every non-transfer route keeps the timeout unchanged, with the same 503 body.
- **Promotion within a shared storage namespace is no longer blocked by its own source repository** (#3266). The #2511 cross-repository write guard excluded only the *target* from its ownership query, but a promote copies the **source** artifact's content-addressed `storage_key` into the target — so on a shared-namespace (cloud) backend the source came back as an "other owner" and every `generic-staging → generic-local` promotion failed with `409 ... already owned by another repository`. The single, bulk, and approval-execute copy paths now pass both ends of the promotion as legitimate owners. The guard's actual job is untouched: a *third* repository holding that key still blocks the write, repo-isolated (filesystem) backends are still skipped, and direct uploads still allow only the writing repository.
- **Deleting a user no longer fails with "db operation failed" when they created migration jobs or signing keys** (#2878, migration 200). `created_by UUID REFERENCES users(id)` in `020_migration_tables.sql` and `027_signing_keys.sql` had no `ON DELETE` clause, which Postgres defaults to `NO ACTION` (RESTRICT), so the `DELETE FROM users` aborted with a foreign-key violation surfaced as an opaque 500 — the OIDC accounts in #2875 could never be removed. Every `REFERENCES users(id)` now carries a deliberate disposition: **`SET NULL`** for provenance/attribution columns (`source_connections`, `migration_jobs`, `signing_keys`, `signing_key_audit`, `promotion_history`, `cve_history`), which keeps the audit rows themselves and matches the pattern already used everywhere else in the schema; **`CASCADE`** for the `NOT NULL` `user_id` of transient upload-session rows (`oci_upload_sessions`, `upload_sessions`, `incus_upload_sessions`), mirroring `083_download_tickets_cascade.sql`. Cascading the audit tables was rejected — deleting a user must not erase who signed a key or approved a promotion. Should a future table reintroduce a bare reference, the delete now returns `409` naming the blocking relation instead of "db operation failed".
- **One oversized federated group name no longer drops all of a user's groups** (#2835). `groups.name` is `VARCHAR(255)`, and `sync_federated_groups_to_local_groups` ran every group in a single transaction with no length check: an IdP that claimed one group name longer than 255 characters aborted the whole transaction (`value too long for type character varying(255)`), so **none** of the user's groups synced — including the legitimate `id_token` ones — while login itself still succeeded, leaving the user silently unauthorized. Names that cannot fit are now rejected before any statement is issued (measured in characters, matching `varchar` semantics, so multi-byte names are not over-counted) and logged as a skip, and each remaining group's DB work runs inside its own `SAVEPOINT` so a residual per-group failure rolls back only that group. Blank claim entries are skipped exactly as before, and the #2759 ownership refusal for name-colliding operator-managed groups is unchanged.
- **An Incus finalize left in flight by a pre-#3072 server is refused instead of double-appending** (#3083, migration 201). #3072's seal-by-rename closed the reclaim double-append for all new upload sessions, but a session already in `finalizing` under pre-#3072 code and reclaimed *after* the upgrade could still append the completing request's body a second time: old code appended it straight onto the staging file and could advance `bytes_received` past it, which on disk is indistinguishable from a legitimately committed session. `incus_upload_sessions.finalize_phase` is that missing bit — current code stamps it on every write that moves a session into `finalizing`, so a `finalizing` row that still has `NULL` there can only have come from the old path. Such a row is no longer reclaimed once its lease has lapsed; the finalize returns `409` telling the operator to cancel the session and re-upload, rather than publishing a corrupt image. The refusal is deliberately scoped to a **lapsed** lease: during a rolling upgrade an un-upgraded replica may still be actively finalizing one of these sessions under a live lease, and that finalize completes correctly under the old algorithm, so a concurrent retry keeps the ordinary "finalize already in progress" response instead of being told to throw the upload away. New sessions never reach this state, so the refusal is bounded to the upgrade itself.
- **A virtual PyPI repository now describes a distribution the same way the direct repository does** (#2748). Three emitters render a Simple index for a virtual repo, and which one runs depends on whether a remote member happened to answer — a fetch miss is a silent `debug!` — so the *same URL* changed shape from request to request. Concretely: (1) the PEP 691 union emitter never set the PEP 658/714 `core-metadata` flag on local-member wheels, though both the direct emitter and the virtual **HTML** emitter did, so through a virtual repository `pip`/`uv` lost the metadata fast path and re-downloaded whole wheels to read `Requires-Dist`; (2) the #2937 ownership filter's HTML rebuild emitted a bare `<a href>`, discarding every `data-*` attribute — so a Case-A platform wheel (the windows build of a version the local owner already ships) lost `data-requires-python`, offering an incompatible build to every interpreter, and lost `data-yanked`, presenting a **withdrawn release as an ordinary resolution candidate**; its JSON sibling only filters entries and always kept both, so the two representations of one index disagreed about installability and about yanking; (3) `versions` was ordered lexicographically on both virtual emitters (`1.10` before `1.9`), regressing the PEP 440 ordering #3106 established for the direct path. The local-entry JSON builder and the anchor rebuilder are now single implementations shared by the direct and virtual paths, so the emitters cannot drift again, and version ordering goes through one PEP 440 comparator. Sharing the anchor rebuilder also gives the ownership filter the proxy rebuild's filename-metacharacter rejection, which it did not have. No change to the #1600/#2311 dependency-confusion boundary or to #2937's Case-A/Case-B decision — a remote-only version of a locally-owned name stays suppressed unless a PEP 708 `tracks` declaration or a member re-ranking permits the union.
- **No more native Basic-auth popup over the login screen when guest access is disabled** (#2936, #3082). With `AK_GUEST_ACCESS_ENABLED=false`, the guest-access guard answered every unauthenticated request — including the web UI's own `fetch()` calls — with `401` + `WWW-Authenticate: Basic`, and browsers respond to that challenge by raising their native credential dialog before the user ever sees the login/OIDC page. The guard (and the optional-auth middleware's invalid-credential 401) now classify browser-originated requests via a pure header check — any Fetch Metadata header (`Sec-Fetch-Mode`/`Sec-Fetch-Site`, forbidden headers that every modern browser sends and no package client does) or an `Accept` listing `text/html` — and omit the `Basic` (and cargo-only `Cargo`) challenge for them, keeping the `Bearer` challenge for RFC 7235 compliance. Browsers therefore land on the web UI's login/OIDC flow directly. Package-manager clients (pip, npm, docker, cargo, maven, conan, …) are never classified as browsers and keep the byte-identical challenge set they had before, so credential retry semantics are unchanged.
- **An OIDC provider seeded from `OIDC_*` env vars no longer outlives its configuration** (#2819, migration 197). `bootstrap_oidc_from_env` created/reconciled a provider row on boot, but removing `OIDC_ISSUER`/`OIDC_CLIENT_ID`/`OIDC_CLIENT_SECRET` and restarting left that row enabled: the login page kept advertising a flow that could no longer complete while `local_login_enabled` stayed `false` because an enabled provider row still existed — locking operators out of the UI. Env-seeded rows are now marked (`oidc_configs.env_seeded`), and a boot without the env vars disables — never deletes — any still-enabled marked row, restoring local login; re-adding the env vars re-enables it through the normal reconcile path. Admin ownership is respected in both directions: rows created via the admin API are never touched (the column defaults to `false`), and editing or re-enabling a previously env-seeded provider through the admin API clears the marker so later boots leave it alone.
- **A backup run whose durable claim is lost now aborts instead of finishing a second archive** (#3084). The claim-renewal loop added in #3072 stopped renewing when ownership was lost, but never told the worker already writing the archive: under a DB partition (or any renewal failure during a long backup) the original replica kept writing while another replica legitimately reclaimed the occurrence — one `backup_schedule_runs` row, two archives in storage. The renewal loop now hands the scheduler a cancellation token that fires the moment a renewal attempt reports ownership loss, and the backup worker observes it between chunks of work — each table export, each artifact read, and (decisively) before the final archive write. Transient renewal errors do not cancel — the claim is still held until its TTL — and dropping the renewal guard only stops the heartbeat, never the worker.
- **Backup and restore now leave audit events, the manifest carries a real checksum, and `BackupType` is honored** (#3011). Four gaps in the same subsystem: (1) `BACKUP_STARTED/COMPLETED/FAILED` and `RESTORE_STARTED/COMPLETED/FAILED` were defined and classified but never emitted — every backup and every restore, the highest-privilege operations in the product, ran unlogged; they are now written (best-effort) from the service so both the API and the scheduler paths are covered, and the restore handler records the requesting user. (2) The manifest shipped `checksum: ""` and `total_size_bytes: 0` in every archive ever produced; it now records the payload byte total and a SHA-256 over every entry's name and content, and the restore path verifies it before touching anything — a corrupted archive is refused outright, while pre-existing archives (empty checksum) restore as before. This closes the checksum half of the archive-integrity gap called out in the 1.7.5 restore-allowlist advisory (GHSA-95fx-g94v-8jqg); archive **signing** remains tracked in #3373. (3) A `metadata` backup no longer silently produces a byte-identical full archive: it captures the database dump and manifest without artifact bytes, and an `incremental` backup without a `since` anchor is rejected at creation instead of masquerading as incremental; incremental *schedules* anchor on the schedule's previous run automatically. (4) `ak_backup_operations_total` / `ak_backup_duration_seconds` are now emitted for API-triggered backups too, not only scheduled ones.
- **The password-expiry notification claim is heartbeated while SMTP runs** (#3086). The claim taken before the send had a fixed 300s expiry and no renewal, so an SMTP attempt that outlived the TTL let a second replica reclaim the row and email the same user again. The claim now gets the same token-fenced heartbeat the curation lease and backup runs use; the TTL remains the failover boundary for a dead worker.
- **The local `peer_instances` row follows `PEER_INSTANCE_NAME` / `PEER_PUBLIC_ENDPOINT` on every boot** (#2832). The row was seeded once at first boot and never reconciled again — `peer_instance_identity` was updated on restart but the Peers UI kept showing the stale first-boot name/endpoint (typically `artifact-keeper-local` / `http://localhost:8080`) forever. The `is_local` row is now reconciled from env on every boot, in place — matched by `is_local`, not by name — so a rename keeps the row's id and its repository assignments.
- **Cargo endpoints answer under the documented `/api/cargo` prefix** (#3000). The generated/documented cargo client config points the sparse registry index at `sparse+{base}/api/cargo/{repo}/`, but the backend only mounted the cargo router at `/cargo`, so cargo's very first fetch — `/api/cargo/{repo}/config.json` — 404'd and every publish against a freshly created repo failed with `config.json not found`. The full cargo router is now additionally mounted at `/api/cargo` (same visibility middleware, same upload limit), so `config.json`, the sparse index files, downloads and publishes resolve under both prefixes.
- **npm Virtual repositories merge every member's packument instead of first-match-wins** (#2844). The virtual metadata walk returned as soon as ANY member knew the package, so a hosted member holding only a fork build (e.g. `19.2.25-myorg.1`) masked the proxy member entirely and `npm install pkg@19.2.25` failed with "no matching version" even though the proxy member carried that release — deleting the fork artifact "fixed" it. Member contributions are now merged in priority order: `versions`, `dist-tags` and `time` federate across members with the higher-priority member winning per key, other packument fields keep the highest-priority member's values, per-member scope policies and age-gate filtering apply to each contribution exactly as before, and an unreachable member is still just a miss. The version-metadata path (`GET /{pkg}/{version}`) resolves through the same merge, so a version that only a lower-priority member carries now resolves there too.
- **Helm charts push with a plain `helm cm-push chart.tgz <repo-url>`** (#2941). The ChartMuseum `cm-push` plugin constructs its push URL as `{host}/api{repo_path}/charts`, so for a repository URL of `{host}/helm/{repo}` it POSTs to `/api/helm/{repo}/charts` — not the native `/helm/{repo}/api/charts` — and the push 404'd unless the non-obvious `--context-path` flag was supplied. The upload and delete handlers are now also mounted under the plugin's default shape (`POST /api/helm/{repo}/charts`, `DELETE /api/helm/{repo}/charts/{name}/{version}`).
- **The NuGet flat-container repair re-materializes the row's own storage object** (#2921). The #2919 streaming repair warms the shared proxy cache under `v3/flatcontainer/...`, which is a different key namespace from the row's `artifacts.storage_key`, and since #1278 proxy-cached content is not recorded in `artifacts` — so nothing ever healed the row, and every subsystem that reads `storage_key` directly (vulnerability scanning, quality gates, peer replication, promotion, signing, backup/export, the NuGet V2 OData download) kept seeing a missing blob permanently. When the repair finds a committed proxy-cache body, it now copies it back to the row's storage key — no second upstream pull — and serves from storage like the primary path; a cold cache still streams from upstream and heals on the next request. Entries that are negative-cached, age-gate-held, or content-coded are never copied; the digest-verified buffered repair (#2929) already wrote back and is unchanged.
- **Remote NuGet/Chocolatey metadata caching survives large OData queries** (#3291). A Chocolatey `choco search` sends OData queries whose sanitized cache segment exceeded the 255-byte filesystem component limit, so every sidecar write failed with `File name too long (os error 36)`, the entry was treated as a permanent cache miss, and each search/list re-queried upstream. Segments over 200 bytes are now truncated and suffixed with a SHA-256 prefix of the raw query — deterministic, collision-safe, and inside every filesystem's component limit — while shorter segments keep their exact historical keys so existing cache entries stay hits.
- **Artifactory source connections ping deployments fronted by the JFrog Platform router** (#2847). The migration connection test probed only the classic `{base}/api/system/ping`, which JFrog Cloud and self-hosted platform installs answer with `404 page not found`; those instances expose the Access service's ping at `{platform_root}/access/api/v1/system/ping` instead. The ping now falls back through the Access-service shapes (with a trailing `/artifactory` context stripped for the platform-root candidate) and reports reachable on the first success, so dry-run imports no longer fail at the connection test.

### Upgrade note — security grades drop and severity gates tighten in volume on the next rescan

Ungraded findings previously landed at `info`, which is in no `max_severity` block set, carries zero penalty weight, and is invisible to promotion severity counts — so they affected nothing. After this change each ungraded finding is filed at `high`: it now counts against `scan_policies.max_severity` thresholds of `high`, `medium`, and `low`, contributes `high`-level weight to repository security scores, and is counted by promotion rules that read high-severity counts.

Expect this to move **in volume** on the first rescan, not at the margin. In particular, OpenSCAP: XCCDF's default rule severity is `unknown`, and the bundled wrapper emits the literal `unknown` for any rule that declares no severity — on a typical SCAP profile that is **most reported rules**. Repositories with OpenSCAP findings should expect their security grade to drop by one or more bands and, if a `max_severity` policy of `high` or stricter is enabled, downloads/promotions of affected artifacts to start being blocked. Trivy image scans with `UNKNOWN`-severity CVEs move the same way at smaller volume. To triage, acknowledge individual findings (acknowledged findings are excluded from the gate) or grade the rules in your SCAP content; do not widen the policy threshold past `high` unless you intend to stop blocking known-high findings too.

## [1.7.5] - 2026-08-14

### Security

- **Backup restore rejects tables outside the export allowlist** (GHSA-95fx-g94v-8jqg) - the restore path never consulted `ALLOWED_EXPORT_TABLES`, so a crafted archive could drive inserts into tables the backup format never legitimately carries (`signing_keys`, `audit_log`). Restore now ingests only the allowlisted set and rejects anything else. **Read the scope carefully:** the allowlist itself contains `users`, `roles`, `user_roles`, `permission_grants` and `api_tokens` - the identity and authorization tables - because a backup that could not restore them would not be a backup. This fix narrows *which tables* an archive can reach; it does **not** make a hostile archive safe, and an operator who restores a tampered archive can still be handed an attacker-controlled admin user and API token. **The real control is archive integrity, and it is not yet implemented**: `BackupManifest.checksum` is written empty and never verified on restore, so restore trusts archive contents as much as it trusts the database. Treat backup archives as privileged material - restore only archives you produced and can vouch for, and store them somewhere a tamperer cannot reach. Signing and checksum verification are tracked in #3373.
- **Percent-encoded repository keys can no longer bypass repository visibility** (GHSA-fv45-mwhh-q23r) - `repo_visibility_middleware` evaluated the raw (still-encoded) path segment while handlers resolve the decoded key, so an authenticated caller with no grant could read a private repo via e.g. `/maven/privat%65/...`; the middleware now percent-decodes before lookup and fails closed with an existence-hiding 404 when no repository matches.
- **Rate limiting no longer trusts the leftmost X-Forwarded-For entry** (GHSA-8jm4-4x6c-6787) - behind an appending trusted proxy, a client-supplied XFF prefix previously controlled the resolved client IP (bucket rotation, exemption spoofing); the resolver now walks right-to-left and selects the first non-trusted, parseable hop. No behavior change for the default compose deployment (Caddy as the single trusted proxy); multi-hop deployments must list every trusted hop in `RATE_LIMIT_TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS`.


### Added

- **Artifact responses carry the uploader** (#3271). `artifacts.uploaded_by` has been on the row since the column was added and was surfaced on `ArtifactVersionResponse` by #2397, but the artifact list/detail responses the web UI actually reads never serialized it, so the artifact detail popup had no uploader to show. `ArtifactResponse` now carries `uploaded_by` (the user id, always serialized, `null` for anonymous uploads and for proxy-cached objects, which have no `artifacts` row) alongside `uploaded_by_username`, resolved server-side so the UI does not have to follow a bare UUID with an admin-only user lookup. The name is resolved in one batched query per listing page — never one per row — and is best-effort: a lookup failure omits names rather than failing the listing, and a deleted uploader keeps its id with a `null` name. The last-downloaded half of the same UI request needs no backend work; `GET /api/v1/artifacts/{id}/stats` already returns `last_downloaded`.
- **OCI 1.1 Referrers API** (#3108). `GET /v2/<name>/referrers/<digest>` now returns an image index of every manifest in the repository whose `subject` points at the digest — empty index (200) when there are none, `400 DIGEST_INVALID` for a malformed digest, `artifactType` filtering with the `OCI-Filters-Applied` header, and descriptors carrying `artifactType`/`annotations` per the distribution spec. Subject edges are recorded at manifest-PUT time in the new `oci_manifest_subjects` table (same transaction as the tag upsert) and removed when the digest's last tag entry is deleted; a subject-carrying push now answers the `OCI-Subject` header, which is how oras/cosign discover Referrers support instead of falling back to the tag scheme. Closes the remaining distribution-spec v1.1.0 conformance content-discovery failures.

### Changed

- **The proxy/OCI inline scan gate no longer blocks on `Info`-severity findings** (#3243 stage 2). `ProxyScanVerdict::is_vulnerable()` was `findings_count > 0`, severity-blind, so every Debian/Ubuntu-derived base image — which carries a long tail of `Negligible` CVEs, all bucketed at `Info` — produced a `vulnerable` verdict and a 403, making fail-closed OCI proxying unusable against ordinary images. The verdict now counts findings **above** `Info`: `Negligible`/`info`/`informational` findings are still scanned, recorded in the verdict row, and reported, but they no longer 403 a pull. This matches Harbor, which documents `negligible` as never blocking at any threshold. **Upgrade note:** repositories with `scan_on_proxy` enabled whose only blocking findings were `Info`-severity start serving those digests on the next scan (stored verdicts re-record on their next freshness re-scan, or within the 30-day TTL). Findings that carry any graded severity — `low` and above — block exactly as before.
- **`scan_configs.block_on_policy_violation` and `severity_threshold` are now enforced on the proxy/OCI inline scan gate** (#3243 stage 3, #3246). Both columns shipped in migration 022 with full CRUD, UI, and CLI plumbing but no enforcement reader anywhere — the UI promise "findings at or above this severity count as a policy violation" was never kept. `block_on_policy_violation` (default **false**) is now the explicit per-repo opt-in: when set, a `vulnerable` verdict blocks the pull **only** when its highest observed severity is at or above the repo's `severity_threshold`; a verdict whose severity is known and strictly below the threshold serves. The gate fails closed on every ambiguous shape — a legacy verdict row with no stored `max_severity`, an unparseable severity token, or an unreadable `scan_configs` row all still block — and the threshold applies uniformly at all four consumption sites: the shared per-format verdict gate (PyPI/npm/OCI manifests), the inline scan outcome, and both OCI blob re-block queries (single-repo and virtual member-spanning), with virtual repositories taking the stricter-of-two gate over the virtual's and the member's configs so a virtual can never become the lax route. **Upgrade note:** a repository on column defaults changes nothing — the opt-in defaults to false, so the historical block-on-any-finding posture is preserved byte-for-byte. Only repositories where an operator had explicitly set `block_on_policy_violation = true` begin getting the documented threshold semantics (their previously-inert `severity_threshold`, default `high`, becomes live).
- **`trivy filesystem` scans no longer drop ungraded (`UNKNOWN`) CVEs at the scanner invocation** (#3296). Three Trivy producers — the hosted-artifact filesystem scanner, the incus rootfs scanner, and the scanner-adapter's filesystem mode — pinned a `--severity CRITICAL,HIGH,MEDIUM,LOW` allowlist, so a CVE Trivy could not grade was filtered out before the report was written: no `scan_findings` row, no count contribution, invisible to every gate, score, and triage workflow, and unrecoverable by any operator-side policy because the data was discarded at the CLI. All three now share one severity list (`TRIVY_FS_SEVERITY_LIST` in the backend; the adapter's `fsSeverity` mirrors it) that includes `UNKNOWN`, matching the adapter's image path, which always admitted it. **Upgrade note:** this is fail-closed in direction — strictly more findings reported, so security scores can drop and finding counts rise on the next rescan of content carrying ungraded CVEs. How an ungraded finding is *classified* once reported is #3306 (tracked separately).
- **`.trivyignore` entries can now be retired** (#3309). Release-preflight check 1 required main's suppression set to be a superset of every active `release/*` branch's, which made the set monotonic: a suppression provably dead for main's images could never be deleted while any release branch still listed it, and nine dead tokens were stuck on main in `RETIRED` prose comments. Check 1 now accepts an explicit `# RETIRED: <token>` tombstone line as accounting for a release-branch token, so a deliberate, measured removal is a reviewable one-line diff the gate reads by machine — while a token a release branch has and main neither lists nor tombstones (the #3039 forward-port miss) still hard-fails, and a token listed both live and retired fails as a contradiction. The eight measured-dead tokens are deleted and tombstoned; `CVE-2026-46600` stays live because it was re-measured as present in the grype v0.117.0 binary's Go stdlib. Both directions are covered by `scripts/ci/test-release-preflight.sh`.
- **The scheduled `cargo audit` job is renamed to "Security audit report (non-gating)"** (#3310). It shared the display name "Security Scan" with docker-publish's hard publish gate while being structurally unable to fail (every step ends in `|| true`), so "Security Scan is green/red" was ambiguous between a meaningful gate and a report generator. The stale "red by design until #1611/#1609 lands" soft-fail comments on the cache-correctness and concurrency e2e suites (both issues long closed) are replaced with named waivers pointing at the real, current blocker: both harnesses abort at repo creation because the SSRF guard rejects their docker-internal mock upstreams (#3363); the suites flip to blocking when that is fixed.

### Added

- **Admin endpoints to inspect and invalidate a proxy scan verdict by digest** (#3244, split from #3105). `GET /api/v1/admin/proxy-scan-verdicts/{digest}` returns every stored `proxy_scan_results` row for a content digest (accepts `sha256:`-prefixed or bare hex); `DELETE` removes them, forcing re-assessment on the next pull — previously the only recourse for a stale or false-positive verdict was raw `psql` against the production database. Both routes are instance-admin only (the verdict store is content-addressed and global across repositories and tenants, so no repo-scoped principal should hold this lever), and the deletion is recorded in the audit log with the discarded verdict summary. Deliberately **not** a waiver: a still-flagged image re-records `vulnerable` on its next scan; risk-acceptance semantics (scoped, expiring waivers à la Harbor CVE allowlists / Xray ignore rules) remain a separate feature.

### Fixed

- **Maven grouped components for remote/proxy repositories list their files again** (#3270, #3265). The grouped view (`?group_by=maven_component`) reconstructs a remote repository's components from the `packages` catalog, which carries no per-file detail, so every remote component shipped `artifact_files: []` while hosted ones were populated from the `artifacts` table. The web UI renders one row per file (with a special-cased POM row), so a proxied component rendered as an empty shell — no POM, no jar, an empty "Files" tab. The individual cached objects are already enumerated in the `proxy_cache_artifacts` catalog (migration 159), so the file list is now recovered from there for **only the page being returned**: one bounded `LIKE ANY` over this page's GAV directory prefixes, capped at 64 files per component, so the query stays O(page) no matter how large the proxy cache grows. Attribution is re-checked in Rust rather than trusted from SQL — `_` is legal in a Maven artifactId and is a single-character wildcard under `LIKE`, so an over-fetched row is dropped instead of being attributed to a neighbouring component — and paths nested below the GAV directory are excluded, matching the hosted listing's one-row-per-file view.
- **Proxied Maven artifacts show their real coordinates and download count** (#3265). A remote repository's flat listing is reconstructed from the cache catalog, which stores only a path, so a proxy-cached row reported `name` as the bare filename (`guava-33.6.0-jre.jar` where a hosted row says `guava`) and no version at all. The UI reads those two fields as the artifactId and version, so a proxied artifact showed a blank version, the wrong artifactId in the package view, and a "copy install command" snippet built from those wrong coordinates. Maven-family remote listings now derive `(artifactId, version)` from the cache path, matching what a hosted upload of the same GAV reports. `maven-metadata.xml` and its checksum sidecars are deliberately excluded — they sit one directory *above* the version directory, where the coordinate parser would read the artifactId segment as the version — while a checksum sidecar of a real asset resolves normally, exactly as a hosted repository lists it. Separately, the download counter stayed at zero because the Maven remote branch was the one proxying format that never called `record_proxy_download`; pypi, npm and everything routed through the shared `try_remote_or_virtual_download` already did. It now records on both of its exits (the sha1-sidecar-gated fetch and the plain streaming fetch), HEAD-guarded and best-effort as elsewhere, and the recorded count is surfaced on the listing through one batched lookup per page instead of the hardcoded `0`.
- **OCI: upload-status probe (`GET /v2/<name>/blobs/uploads/<uuid>`) returned 405** (#3275). The catch-all dispatcher had no `("GET", "uploads")` arm, so clients recovering a chunked upload after a dropped connection or a 416 could not retrieve the current valid offset — docker/buildkit fell back to restarting the whole blob. The probe now answers `204 No Content` with `Location`, `Range: 0-<end>` and `Docker-Upload-UUID`, per the spec's active-upload contract.
- **OCI: setup and demo guards emitted REST-shaped JSON on `/v2`** (#3284). The global `setup_guard` and `demo_guard` layers sit in front of the nested `/v2` router and answered 403 with REST bodies (`{"error": ...}`) that docker/oras cannot render — a fresh unconfigured instance reported a `docker login` failure with no usable message. Refusals on the OCI surface are now the distribution-spec envelope `{"errors":[{"code":"DENIED","message":...}]}` via a shared middleware helper; REST routes keep their existing bodies.
- **OCI: `HEAD /v2/<name>/blobs/<digest>` skipped the scan re-block** (#3258). The #3003 blob re-block (a blob belonging to an image with a `vulnerable` verdict must not serve) had exactly one call site — `handle_get_blob` — so HEAD answered `200` with an exact `Content-Length` for content whose GET was `403`, leaking the existence and size of blocked content and making the two verbs disagree on the same resource. The check is extracted into a shared `enforce_blob_scan_reblock` helper (including the #3245 `scan_on_proxy` scoping and the #3023 virtual-member spanning) and applied to both verbs.
- **OCI proxy: Docker schema1 (v2s1) manifests evaded the scan-on-proxy gate** (#3024). The runnable-image predicate keys on a `config` descriptor, which v2s1 (`schemaVersion: 1` / `fsLayers`) predates — so the one legacy-runnable manifest shape proxied through a scan-enabled Remote repository `200` unscanned while every schema2/OCI variant was gated. The scanner cannot reassemble a v2s1 image (no config blob), so under a scan-on-proxy policy the deprecated schema is now refused outright (`403 DENIED`); with scanning disabled it still proxies unchanged.
- **Upstream `Content-Encoding` is re-declared on the remaining verbatim buffered forwards** (#3273). The Pub registry's non-JSON metadata pass-through (`proxy_pub_meta_get`'s fallback arm) and the buffered slow path of `proxy_fetch_or_redirect` both served upstream bytes verbatim while dropping the coding the upstream declared — the same #3211/#3260 shape at two sites that fix deliberately left out of scope. Both now thread the coding through and re-declare it (RFC 9110 §8.4), with `Content-Length` describing the coded bytes actually sent; the Pub JSON-rewrite arm keeps dropping it, since the bytes it emits are its own.
- **goproxy `@v/list` and `@latest` handle upstreams that store a `Content-Encoding`** (#3280). `@v/list` lossily decoded coded bytes into a run of U+FFFD and served it as `200 text/plain`; `@latest` failed the JSON parse on the coded bytes and returned 404 for every coded-upstream module. The list/latest fetch now reports the upstream coding, both parse paths strip a recognised coding through the shared bounded decoder (decompression-bomb budget included) and fail closed with 502 on an unrecognised or corrupt one, and `@latest` — which forwards the upstream document verbatim after gating — re-declares the coding on its response.
- **Virtual verbatim metadata forwards serve the member upstream's `Content-Type`** (#3281). `resolve_virtual_metadata` discarded the member's `Content-Type`, so all four Virtual verbatim forwards (hex `package_info`, rubygems `gem_info` and `quick_spec`, goproxy `.info`/`.mod`) overrode it with a hardcoded literal — a `mix` client asking a Virtual repository for `/packages/{name}` got the upstream's signed **protobuf** labelled `application/json`, where the Remote arm of the same endpoint forwards the type correctly. The transform seam now passes the member's type through, keeping each format literal only as the fallback for a member that declares none.
- **PyPI HTML and PEP 691 JSON proxy caches no longer drift apart** (#3290). The two content-negotiated representations of a Simple project index are cached under separate keys with independent lifetimes, and two seams let them diverge: the stale-entry conditional revalidation probed upstream **without** the `Accept` header the cached variant was fetched under (so the JSON variant's ETag was validated against the HTML representation, RFC 9110 §12.5.1), and explicitly invalidating one representation left the other serving an older snapshot — `uv` (which prefers JSON) reported versions missing that the HTML index already listed. Revalidation now replays the selecting `Accept`, and `POST /:key/cache/invalidate` on a PyPI repository evicts both variants of a Simple-index path in one operation.
