- **A just-cached streaming proxy entry is readable to closely-following requests** (#3335). The streaming tee completes the client response as soon as the last byte is forwarded, but the cache writer still has its publish tail to run (staging copy, ETag pin, sidecar write) — and until the sidecar landed, a request for the same path classified the entry as a miss and opened its own upstream fetch. That both amplified upstream traffic behind a completing fetch and, on mutable paths, re-served whatever the upstream returned inside the window. Streaming publishes now register in an in-process registry; a cache read that misses while a tail-phase publish is in flight waits (bounded, 5 s) for the writer and re-reads the sidecar — dropping any memoised miss from the metadata LRU — instead of refetching. Reads during the body transfer are unaffected (the single-flight coordinator already coalesces those), and a rejected write still self-heals as before.
- **Proxy scan verdicts now invalidate when the CVE database updates, not only when the scanner binary is upgraded** (#3287). The freshness check (`verdict_is_fresh`, #2976) documents its reuse rule as "a CVE-DB bump changes the version string", but the stored token was the scanner **CLI** version alone (`grype-0.83.0`) — and Grype ships database updates daily while the CLI changes rarely, so a withdrawn advisory kept an image blocked (and a newly published one kept a clean verdict serving) for the full 30-day TTL. The stored token now composes the CLI version with the vulnerability-database build date — `grype-0.83.0+db-2026-08-10` from `grype db status`'s `Built` line, `trivy-0.62.1+db-2026-08-10` from `trivy --version`'s `Vulnerability DB: UpdatedAt` block — so the practical stale window drops from ~30 days to ~1. A failed DB probe degrades to the bare CLI token (TTL-only freshness, exactly the previous behavior), and existing stored verdicts mismatch the new token shape once, forcing a single re-scan per digest after upgrade — the self-healing direction.
- **Attestation verification authenticates the Rekor `integratedTime` by verifying the Signed Entry Timestamp** (#3231). The SET is the log's signature over a tlog entry's `(body, integratedTime, logIndex, logID)`, and it is the only thing that binds `integratedTime` — it is neither part of `canonicalizedBody` nor part of the Merkle leaf, so the inclusion-proof glue #3199 added does not cover it, and `sigstore` 0.14.0's `Verifier::verify_digest` skips it outright (`TODO(tnytown) SET verification`). That made `integratedTime` a value the bundle's author chose, and `integratedTime` is exactly what step 7 of `verify_digest` compares the signing certificate's validity window against, so "the certificate was valid when it signed" was trivially satisfiable by anyone already producing the bundle. Verification now runs the SET against the Rekor log key from the pinned trusted root, as an eighth mandatory check (`Check::RekorSet`) ahead of the inclusion proof, and **fails closed in both directions**: a SET that does not verify is a rejection, and so is a tlog entry carrying no `inclusionPromise` at all, since an absent SET means an unauthenticated timestamp rather than an acceptable omission. Every PyPI PEP 740 bundle observed carries one, and PyPI is the only format this verifier accepts. Not hand-rolled crypto: the payload is the field set of the crate's own `cosign::bundle::Payload`, canonicalised with the same RFC 8785 (JCS) library the crate uses for the same payload in its private cosign path, verified with the same `CosignVerificationKey`. The red-team fixture whose only mutation is a rewritten `integratedTime` is now rejected by the SET check directly, and `pypi-whl-bundle--forged-set-and-proof` now fails at the SET rather than incidentally at the proof.
- **A persisted attestation verification survives re-evaluation** (#3230). Migration 195 added `attestation_state` / `_identity` / `_issuer` / `_owner` / `_verified_at` / `_error` to `curation_packages` and the scheduler wrote them, but nothing read them back: `CurationPackage` did not carry the fields, so the `SELECT *` behind every catalog read decoded and dropped them, and the trust decision lived only in the memory of the tick that ran the verifier. Any later re-evaluation of the same row — an operator resetting a package to `pending`, a bulk re-evaluate, a manual sync — therefore lost `verified = true`, fell back to the fail-safe Flag, and silently returned an `approved` package to `review`; it also re-downloaded the whole distribution from the upstream, because there was no cached verdict to consult. The columns are now the source of truth: both the scheduler's on-demand loop and `re_evaluate_pending` read the record and derive the trust marker from it, under guards that all fail toward re-verifying rather than trusting — the record must say `verified`, carry all three certificate-bound values, name an issuer **still** on the current allowlist (so narrowing the allowlist takes effect at the next evaluation, not the next re-verification), be no older than 30 days, and predate nothing: a row re-ingested from the upstream since the record was written is verified again. The record is also surfaced on the curation package API responses, so "which packages verified, and against which certificate identity" is answerable without querying the database.
- **The on-demand curation ingestion seam is covered by tests and capped** (#3233). #3199 moved the PyPI seam to after the serve call returns, because in its original position it ran ahead of the repository access check and ahead of any upstream contact — an unauthenticated caller could write `curation_packages` rows for packages that do not exist, into a staging repo belonging to another tenant. That relocation shipped without a test; the predicate now lives in `proxy_helpers::response_admits_ondemand_ingest` and is pinned in both directions (2xx and the #1555 presigned `307` ingest; 401/403/404/451/5xx never do), alongside a DB-backed positive control that a served download enqueues exactly one pending row per curating staging repo and a repeat download adds none. Proxy-driven ingestion is additionally bounded: a staging repo at `MAX_PENDING_ONDEMAND_ROWS` (5000) pending rows drops **new** rows with a warning while continuing to refresh existing ones, so proxy traffic cannot inflate the catalog without limit or push real packages behind a head-of-line backlog that `MAX_PENDING_PER_TICK = 500` drains 500 rows at a time.
- **Blob GC's readiness gate can be opened again on registries serving multi-arch images** (#3285). The gate (`any_live_manifest_missing_refs`) was strictly wider than the backfill candidate set: it counted the children of every tagged index, while the candidate query selected only *tagged* digests and therefore never selected an index child, which carries no tag of its own. A child without `manifest_blob_refs` rows pinned the gate `true` with no way to clear it — the startup backfill logged `candidates_scanned=0 edges_inserted=0` while `BLOB_GC_ENABLED=true` stayed a permanent no-op, so soft-deleted artifacts were reclaimed from the database by the storage GC while their blobs stayed in object storage forever. On the reporting instance 365 manifests held it closed and an external SQL + S3 `DeleteObjects` job had to reclaim 2.27 TiB by hand. Both queries are now derived from one shared relation, so the gate can only be held closed by something the backfill also selects. That relation adds index children, but only where a **live `artifacts` row in the same repository** sits at the child's `oci-manifests/<digest>` key — the locality proof that this repository actually committed that manifest body, which is what previously made a bare `oci_manifest_refs` edge unsafe to backfill (on a shared cloud namespace it would have imported another repository's manifest). A child that is merely *referenced* is now in neither set: a proxy cache legitimately holds only the architecture it was asked for, so an unfetchable child can neither be backfilled nor gate — and with no manifest body there are no local blobs attributable to it for a sweep to reclaim. Nested indexes are excluded from the child arm by the same structural guard #1409 applied to tagged indexes. When the gate is closed, the scheduler now names up to 20 of the offending `digest@repository` pairs in its per-tick warning, so a stuck gate is diagnosable without reconstructing the query by hand.
- **Storage accounting no longer double-counts OCI layer bytes** (#3286). `REPO_OBJECT_UNION_SQL` unioned live `artifacts` rows with `oci_blobs`, on the premise that a Docker/OCI repository contributes only a few KiB of manifests through `artifacts`. It does not: `artifacts.size_bytes` for an image manifest holds `config.size` plus the sum of `layers[].size` — the aggregate *image* size, deliberately, because GC, quota and scanning enumerate that table — so every layer was counted once through the inflated manifest row and again through `oci_blobs`. The reporter measured `instance_storage_stats.unique_bytes` at 3097 GB against 1.14 TiB of actual bucket usage, a 2.7x over-report on a metric named `unique_bytes` that an operator reasonably reads as deduplicated physical usage. `oci-manifests/%` rows are now excluded from the physical union, so OCI layers are counted exactly once, via `oci_blobs` at their true size. **Upgrade impact: reported storage will drop sharply on OCI-heavy instances after the next stats refresh — that is the correction, not data loss, and no bytes are deleted.** **Quota enforcement is unchanged in both directions**: `RepositoryService::check_quota` reads the live logical `SUM` over `artifacts` and never consulted this accounting layer (#2056 §7), so no repository's effective limit moves. The manifest JSON documents themselves are no longer counted — a deliberate, bounded undercount (~164 MB across 56k manifests on the reporting instance, ~0.01%), since no column holds their true object size; rewriting `artifacts.size_bytes` to fix that was rejected precisely because it *would* have silently loosened every OCI repository's quota. The per-path tree (`PATH_REF_UNION_SQL`) is unchanged: it is a logical view that excludes layer blobs entirely, so it has no double count to correct. The same conflated column also produced the OCI migration reindex's false positives — its cheap pre-read guard compared a 4 MiB *object* cap against the image size and skipped 8,069 otherwise-ordinary manifests with `manifest body exceeds 4194304 bytes (got 322036591); refusing to buffer` — so `oci-manifests/%` rows now skip that pre-read guard and are bounded by the unconditional post-read `body.len()` cap like every other manifest.
- **The proxy-cache catalog backfill repairs the placeholder row it races** (#3305). On a cache serve, `record_proxy_download` is awaited inline on the request and inserts a `size_bytes = 0`, NULL-checksum placeholder so the first download counts (#2537), while `spawn_lazy_catalog_backfill` runs detached and normally *loses* that race. Its `ON CONFLICT` arm bumped only `last_accessed_at`, so the losing backfill returned without ever writing the real size, checksum or content type, and nothing else repaired the row — the #2218/#2270 self-healing was inert for exactly the pre-catalog entries it exists for, and storage accounting read zeros for them. The conflict arm now repairs a row that is still a placeholder (`size_bytes = 0` **and** checksum NULL) and continues to leave every authoritative row untouched, so a genuinely populated entry — including a real zero-byte object, which carries its checksum — is never overwritten by a stale sidecar. The bounding #3289 added is untouched; a skipped backfill still simply retries on the next hit.
- **S3 `get_stream` runs the Artifactory-migration fallback that `get` and `get_range` already performed** (#2927). S3 was the only fallback-capable backend whose streaming read skipped `try_fallback_get` — GCS and Azure both run it — so in a deployment configured with the Artifactory migration path format, an object living only at the legacy key layout was found by the buffered `storage.get()` and reported `NotFound` by `storage.get_stream()`. That mattered because handlers are being converted from buffered to streaming reads (#895 / #1608 / #2192): each conversion silently inherited the gap, turning a warm local read into either a hard 404 or a redundant upstream re-download. The fallback body is buffered and wrapped in a single-item stream, matching GCS and Azure exactly. `try_fallback_get` is gated on `path_format.has_fallback()`, so **nothing changes for deployments not in migration mode, or on any other backend**. A regression test pins the invariant across all three fallback-capable backends so it cannot drift again.
- **Release-preflight check 3 measures the Docker Publish run for the commit being tagged, not the newest run** (#3338). Selecting by recency produced a blocking false red on the v1.7.2 cut when the "latest run" query returned a ten-day-stale run, and it was the wrong question even with a consistent API: a tag is cut on a commit, and only that commit's publish determines whether the release chain stalls. The run is now selected by `head_sha`, and two previously conflated states are reported distinctly and block: *no run for this commit* ("not built yet" — the images a tag would pin do not exist) and *run still in flight* (its verdict does not exist yet). `PREFLIGHT_ALLOW_RED_PUBLISH` continues to override only a measured red.
- **CI fails on duplicate sqlx migration version numbers with a numeric comparison** (#3313). Two migration PRs claiming the same version merge CLEAN with no textual conflict and abort the backend at startup (`_sqlx_migrations_pkey`, #1128); the inline string-prefix dedup in `ci.yml` would additionally have passed `007_x.sql` alongside `7_y.sql`, which sqlx treats as the same version. The check now lives in `scripts/ci/check-duplicate-migrations.sh` — numeric-normalised duplicate detection, an error on unparseable prefixes, and a non-blocking warning on sequence gaps (the renumber-in-flight signature) — runs against the PR merge result as before, and is exercised by a self-test in the shell-tests job so the gate itself cannot rot.
- **The 8.1 MB compiled aarch64 `scanner-adapter` binary is removed from the repository** (#3341). It sat inside `docker/scanner-adapter/`, the exact path set the publish job diffs to decide whether the published version tag may be reused — so any local `go build` that got committed would invalidate the published tag with no source change and no clue in the diff, the same gate that killed the v1.7.2 cut. Nothing consumed it: the image compiles the adapter from source (`COPY docker/scanner-adapter/*.go`), and no other Dockerfile or compose file references it. The path is now gitignored, and `docker/scanner-adapter/VERSION` is bumped to 1.2.4 because the removal itself changes the diffed source set.
- **Repeated PyPI PEP 658 `.whl.metadata` requests are served from the proxy cache** (#3300). `serve_remote_metadata` fetched the sidecar with `fetch_upstream_direct_with_link`, which bypasses the proxy cache in both directions, so the bytes were never stored and no request could ever hit. Every request re-ran the entire upstream resolution: the simple index — read through `proxy_fetch_uncached` to rediscover the distribution's download URL — plus the `.metadata` resource itself, two upstream round-trips for a resource that cannot change. An uncached `uv lock` fan-out requests one sidecar per candidate distribution, so a CI job regenerating several lock files multiplied that across every resolver run. The cache is now probed **before** the fetch target is resolved — behind that resolution a hit would still pay the index read — keyed on `simple/{project}/{dist}.metadata`, which is what `resolve_pypi_remote_fetch_target` already computes and is distinct from the distribution's own key. A miss fetches through `fetch_artifact_with_cache_path_capped`, which stores the body together with its upstream `Content-Encoding`, so a later hit re-emits the coding #3193 established rather than serving coded bytes bare. **Both direct Remote repositories and Virtual members are covered**, because virtual metadata resolution routes through the same function. Separately, the proxy-cache classifier matched `.whl.metadata` against no PyPI distribution extension and therefore gave it the mutable default — a five-minute TTL, and a revalidation against upstream on every request after it, even once the body was stored. A PEP 658 sidecar holds the `METADATA` of an immutable distribution, so it now classifies as immutable, with the suffix stripped ahead of the extension test exactly as the Maven checksum and signature sidecars are. The upstream-`404`-to-wheel-extraction fallback is deliberately unchanged: only the `200` path is cached, so an upstream that advertises metadata it does not serve still re-extracts from its (cached) wheel per request.
- **Corrected swapped GHSA labels** in `backend/tests/security_regression_tests.rs` - the Maven LIKE-escape and IPv6/cloud-metadata SSRF regression tests were annotated with each other's advisory IDs; coverage was always correct, only the comments were wrong.

## [1.7.4] - 2026-08-14

A security-only release. It carries exactly one functional commit on top of 1.7.3 — four repository advisories fixed together (#3347) — plus the version bump. There are **no feature changes and no schema migrations**, so for anyone already on 1.7.3 this is a low-risk upgrade that can be taken quickly. Three of the four advisories need action beyond upgrading — credential rotation for GHSA-78h6, a proxy-cache judgement call for GHSA-qxv7, and token rotation for GHSA-9rqp — and the GHSA-78h6 fix carries one deliberate behaviour change on Docker Hub pull-through; all of it is written up below.

### Upgrade note — credentialed Docker Hub pull-through now authenticates anonymously

This is a consequence of the GHSA-78h6-3wp8-2542 fix rather than a separate decision, and it is the only behaviour change in the release.

Docker Hub splits its registry and its token endpoint across two hosts: pulls go to `registry-1.docker.io` while the realm advertised in its `401` is `https://auth.docker.io/token`. Under the strict origin rule the fix introduces, those are **different origins**, so a Docker Hub remote/proxy repository with configured upstream credentials now performs its token exchange **without them**. Docker Hub does not reject that request — it answers with a valid *anonymous-scope* token — so the failure does not land at the token endpoint where the comment in the fix anticipates it. It lands one hop later, on the manifest retry, and `validate_upstream_status` renders the upstream's `401` as **`502 Upstream returned error status 401`**. That misleading error is the diagnostic signature: if a private Docker Hub pull-through starts answering `502` after this upgrade, this is the cause, and the Docker Hub credential itself is not the problem.

**Am I affected?** Only if you proxy an upstream whose token realm lives on a different host from the registry itself **and** you configured Basic credentials on that repository. Expect two effects: **private images stop being pullable** through that repository, and pulls that previously authenticated now fall under the upstream's **anonymous rate limits** — on a busy CI fleet, the second is what you notice first. Registries advertising a same-origin realm are unaffected and keep authenticating exactly as before: GHCR serves pulls from `ghcr.io` and advertises `ghcr.io/token`, as do most self-hosted Harbor, Nexus and Artifactory deployments. Check your own upstreams rather than assuming — the realm host is in the `WWW-Authenticate` header of an unauthenticated `GET` against the upstream's `/v2/`.

There is deliberately **no configuration switch to opt back in**, and no well-known-realm allowlist. The codebase pins credentials to the operator-configured origin everywhere (#2925, #3130), and a cross-host allowance would reopen the advisory for every upstream that has one.

### Upgrade note — what to do beyond upgrading

Three of the four fixes do not fully remediate on their own.

**Rotate upstream Basic credentials that may have been forwarded (GHSA-78h6).** If a remote/proxy repository carried stored upstream *Basic* credentials and its upstream could have advertised a realm on a host you do not control, those credentials may have reached a third party; Basic credentials are replayable indefinitely, and where the realm was plain `http://` they also crossed the wire in cleartext. Upgrading closes the channel but cannot un-send them. Repositories configured with an upstream *bearer* token were never exposed — only the Basic arm forwarded. The assessment is a configuration review, not a log search: enumerate the remote/proxy repositories carrying stored upstream credentials and judge each upstream on whether someone could have controlled its `401` response.

**Decide whether to purge proxy-cache entries admitted before the digest gate existed (GHSA-qxv7).** Nothing in this release evicts or revalidates what is already cached — there is no migration, no startup revalidation, no backfill. An npm tarball, PyPI distribution or Maven release artifact whose bytes disagreed with its published digest and was cached before the upgrade stays cached and keeps being served warm. **It will not age out**: proxied package files classify as `Mutability::Immutable`, so `evaluate` returns `Fresh` unconditionally, the stamped TTL is a ~10-year backstop, and upstream is never consulted for that object again. Eviction is explicit and per-path — enumerate a remote repository's cached entries with `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/artifacts` (the listing carries `path`, `size_bytes` and the stored `checksum_sha256`) and call `POST /api/v1/repositories/{key}/cache/invalidate?path=<path>` for each, which requires `repository:admin`, takes **one path per call** with no prefix, glob or bulk form, and hard-deletes the `__content__` body, the `__cache_meta__.json` sidecar and the `proxy_cache_artifacts` row. The only whole-subtree purge in the codebase (`purge_repo_cache`) is reachable solely from the repository-DELETE background task, so a genuinely clean slate means deleting and recreating the remote repository. Proxy-cache entries are re-fetchable from upstream by definition, so evicting costs bandwidth and a cold first pull rather than data: if you have any reason to suspect a bad entry — a corrupted install report, a flaky upstream or mirror, an upstream incident inside your retention window — evict and let it re-populate under the new gate. With no such signal, taking the fix going forward is defensible. **GHSA-4cw7 needs no purge**, and that is worth stating next to the manual loop above: the proxy caches the raw upstream index body and the page is rebuilt in the handler on every serve including warm cache hits, so a poisoned page already in the cache is sanitized on read the moment this is deployed.

**Also rotate tokens used against third-party plugin routes (GHSA-9rqp).** Every credential presented to a `/ext` URL served by a v2 WASM plugin was copied into that plugin's memory. Keep it in proportion — the sandbox gives a plugin no network and no filesystem, so reading a credential and exfiltrating it are not the same step, and plugin installation is admin-only with `plugins_require_signed` defaulting to `true` — but if you ran a plugin you do not fully control, rotate the tokens of principals that used those routes.

### Upgrade note — exploitation is mostly undetectable after the fact, with one exception

Worth stating plainly, because it changes what a post-upgrade review can achieve.

**GHSA-9rqp leaves no trace at all.** A successful `/ext` request writes no audit entry, never reaches the download-event dispatcher so records no `download_statistics` row, and emits no log line at the shipped `RUST_LOG=info` level. Request metrics label by matched route (`/ext/:format_key/:repo_key/*path`), carrying neither the repository key nor the principal, and no span records the caller. Even against an external reverse-proxy access log, an exploiting request is byte-identical in shape to a legitimate one — same method, same URL — and the only distinguishing fact is which grants the caller held, which nothing records. If the affected configuration was present, treat those repositories' inventories as potentially disclosed.

**GHSA-78h6 is silent on a production deployment.** The realm that was contacted appeared only in a `tracing::debug!` line, and the shipped container images pin `RUST_LOG=info`; no metric, audit row or persisted record retains it, and the bearer-token cache is in-memory and keyed on the realm string. A competent exfiltration returns well-formed token JSON and produces no error line either. A deployment running the development compose defaults (`info,artifact_keeper=debug`) *did* emit that line, so those logs are worth a look if they are still held. Going forward the new cross-origin refusal is logged at `warn` under the `security` target — useful for spotting the Docker Hub change above, but it says nothing about the past.

**GHSA-4cw7 lets you check what is cached now, not what was served.** The proxy caches the raw upstream body, so a currently-cached index page can be inspected directly — on filesystem storage at `<storage>/proxy-cache/<repo_key>/simple/<project>/__content__`, on S3/GCS under the same key prefix at bucket root (the configured `S3_PREFIX` is deliberately not applied to the proxy-cache handle), with `proxy_cache_artifacts` available to enumerate candidates first. Grep those for `<script`, `onerror=` and similar. Upstream response bodies are never logged anywhere, so whether a poisoned page was *ever served* is unanswerable.

**GHSA-qxv7 is the one that does support an offline check.** Every cached entry stores a `checksum_sha256` in its metadata sidecar and in the `proxy_cache_artifacts` catalog, surfaced through `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/artifacts`. For PyPI that value is directly comparable to the `#sha256=` fragment the upstream index advertises for the same filename — pull the listing, fetch the upstream index, diff, with no artifact re-download. For npm and Maven the published digests are SHA-512 and SHA-1, so the stored SHA-256 is not comparable and the cached object must be re-hashed. No record of a *mismatch* was ever kept, before or after the fix, so this compares against today's upstream digest and therefore detects divergence rather than proving what happened at the time: against an upstream that was compromised and has since been corrected it finds the bad entry; against one still serving the attacker's digest it will not.

### Security

- **The `/ext` WASM plugin proxy evaluated the plugin format key as the repository key, so any authenticated caller could read a private repository's artifact inventory** (GHSA-9rqp-mgmw-5879, #3347). The plugin protocol proxy is mounted at `/ext` and routes as `/ext/<format_key>/<repo_key>/…` — the plugin's format key first, the repository second. Every other format route puts the repository key in the second path segment, and `extract_repo_key`, which `repo_visibility_middleware` uses to decide *what it is authorizing*, applied that general rule to `/ext` as well; it returned the **plugin format key**. No repository matched that string, so the middleware fell into its no-repository branch — the branch that exists for routes carrying no repository at all. Anonymous callers were still refused there, but a valid credential inserted the auth extension and ran the handler, so **every check in the repository branch was skipped**: the public/private gate, the repository-scope ceiling `can_access_repo`, and the fine-grained read/write ACLs. The pre-fix handler performed no authorization of its own, so it then looked the real repository up and handed the plugin its full artifact metadata list. **What crossed the boundary is inventory, not content** — `path`, `version`, `content_type`, `size_bytes` and `checksum_sha256` for every live artifact, which the plugin renders into the requested index. The guest receives no artifact bytes (the v2 `handle-request` interface takes only the metadata list, and the runtime grants no network and no preopened directories), there is no write path through the interface, and the `download_base_url` a plugin embeds in its index points at `/api/v1/repositories/{key}/download`, which enforces `require_visible` independently — so those links `404` for an ungranted caller. Two consequences go beyond the headline: a repository-scoped API token was **not** confined, so a token restricted to one repository could read another's inventory; and the format-key mismatch reply named the repository's actual format with a `400` where a missing repository answered `404`, giving any authenticated caller an existence-and-format oracle over **every** repository in the instance — which did not require the format-key match and therefore reached further than the inventory disclosure itself. **Am I affected:** only if you run a WASM plugin exporting the **v2** `request-handler` interface (`capabilities.handle_request`, which is what compiles `compiled_v2` and what `has_handle_request` gates on — a v1 plugin is not enough) **and** at least one non-public repository was created against that plugin's custom format key. The handler requires the target repository's own `format_key` to equal the plugin's, and built-in formats resolve to a `None` format key, so Maven, npm, PyPI, OCI and the other native formats were never reachable this way. Plugin installation is admin-only and `plugins_require_signed` defaults to `true`, so this is not a self-service foothold. The fix has three parts: `extract_repo_key` skips the format-key segment when the first segment is `ext`, so the middleware evaluates the real repository and applies the standard gate (anonymous `401` with the credential challenge, authenticated-without-grant existence-hiding `404`, granted member passes) and the token-scope ceiling and fine-grained ACLs — inert on `/ext` before — now apply; the handler calls `require_visible` itself rather than trusting the middleware to have parsed the path, failing closed with a `500` if the access check errors; and the header forwarding is allowlisted, below. A short `/ext` path yields an empty repository key and 404s at the router before reaching the handler. The regression test is DB-backed and returns early when `DATABASE_URL` is unset, so it proves the fix only in the coverage job that seeds Postgres.
- **The `/ext` proxy no longer copies the caller's credentials into plugin memory** (GHSA-9rqp-mgmw-5879, #3347). A second, distinct exposure in the same handler, fixed in the same commit. `headers_to_pairs` had no filter: **every** client request header was copied into `WasmHttpRequest.headers` and thence into guest memory, so plugin code received the caller's `Authorization` header (`Bearer` JWT, `ApiKey`, or `Basic` user:password), `Cookie`, `Proxy-Authorization`, `X-API-Key`, `X-NuGet-ApiKey` and any custom header on every `/ext` request. Forwarding is now restricted to `FORWARDED_HEADER_ALLOWLIST` — `accept`, `accept-encoding`, `accept-language`, `content-length`, `content-type`, `host`, `if-modified-since`, `if-none-match`, `if-range`, `range`, `user-agent` — the benign protocol and content-negotiation headers a protocol plugin legitimately needs; credential and hop-by-hop headers are never copied. `HeaderMap` names are already lowercase, so the allowlist matches lowercase. Stated precisely, because the remediation advice depends on it: the diff supports "caller credentials entered plugin memory", not "credentials left the host" — the guest has no network and no filesystem, so the only channel out is the host's own stdout via `inherit_stdio`. **Am I affected:** only on the `/ext` precondition above, and the practical risk is a plugin you did not author; rotate the tokens of principals that used those routes.
- **The OCI bearer-realm token exchange no longer forwards stored upstream Basic credentials to a cross-origin realm** (GHSA-78h6-3wp8-2542, #3347). When a proxied fetch receives a `401`, the upstream names its own token endpoint in `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer realm="…"` — **upstream-controlled input**. `exchange_bearer_then` validated that realm with `validate_outbound_url`, which is an SSRF check and nothing more (scheme in `{http, https}`, host present, not a private/loopback/link-local/metadata address), and then passed the repository's decrypted upstream credentials straight into `obtain_bearer_token`, which attaches them as HTTP Basic to the token request. A hostile or compromised registry — or anyone able to tamper with its `401` — could therefore name an attacker-controlled host as its realm and be handed the username and password the operator configured for that upstream. Plain `http://` realms were accepted, so in that case the credentials also crossed the wire in cleartext. Three narrowings are worth stating because they bound the severity honestly: only the **Basic** arm forwarded (a configured `UpstreamAuthType::Bearer` was never matched by that branch and never leaked); **there is no attacker on the Artifact Keeper side** — a caller can trigger the fetch but cannot influence the realm, so exploitation requires a hostile or compromised upstream, a TLS interception of it, or a credentialed proxy repository deliberately pointed at an untrusted registry; and although the advisory is framed around OCI, `exchange_bearer_then` sits on the generic buffered, streaming and conditional proxy fetch paths, so any upstream answering `401` with a `Bearer realm=` challenge reached it. This was **not** also an SSRF primitive — internal addresses were the one thing already covered, both at validation time and by the shared client's per-hop resolver guard — and redirects were already safe, because the HTTP client strips `Authorization` on any cross-host hop. The fix adds `realm_matches_upstream_origin`: credentials follow the realm only when its **scheme, host and port** match the upstream request URL's, with the host lowercased and default ports normalized through `port_or_known_default`, and an unparseable URL on either side matching nothing. The baseline is the operator-configured upstream the request URL was built from, not the URL of whatever answered, so a redirect chain cannot move it. A cross-origin realm gets an **anonymous** token request rather than a hard failure — and a `warn!` under the `security` target with the realm redacted — because anonymous token endpoints answer such requests. There is deliberately no suffix allowance: a subdomain of your upstream is cross-origin. **Am I affected:** only with a remote/proxy repository carrying stored upstream **Basic** credentials whose upstream could have advertised a realm on a host you do not control. **Rotate those credentials if so** — upgrading stops future forwarding but cannot recall what was already sent. See the upgrade note above for the Docker Hub consequence, and the detectability note for why a log search will not answer this for you.
- **Proxied PyPI simple-index pages are rebuilt from parsed anchors instead of forwarding upstream HTML** (GHSA-4cw7-mgqj-hgmg, #3347). `rewrite_upstream_urls` edited the upstream document in place: a regex found `<a … href=…>` and substituted a local URL. Everything else survived. The only element actively removed was `<base>` — stripped to a fixpoint, because removing one can splice surrounding bytes into a new one — since a surviving `<base href>` would re-anchor the rewritten root-relative links back onto the upstream origin. A `<script>`, a `<form>`, an `<iframe>`, arbitrary wrapper markup: all forwarded verbatim and **served from this registry's own origin**. The anchor's own attributes before and after `href` were re-emitted raw as well, so an upstream `onerror=` rode through untouched. Independently, the rewritten `href` was assembled by string interpolation, so upstream-controlled bytes could break out of the attribute they were written into: a single-quoted upstream href whose fragment carries a double quote yields a rewritten anchor with arbitrary attacker-chosen attributes on it. **Two qualifications on severity, because the advisory title invites more than the code supports.** The application's own CSP middleware sets `script-src 'self'` with no `'unsafe-inline'` on every response, and the per-project proxied page carries no tighter handler CSP of its own, so the global default applies and blocks both injected `<script>` blocks and inline event handlers in a CSP-respecting browser — what was genuinely reachable is stored **HTML injection** on a same-origin page, a defence-in-depth failure, not demonstrated script execution or session theft. (The comment in `security_headers.rs` describing a tight `default-src 'none'` for the PyPI simple index refers to the **root** listing, which emits its own CSP; the affected per-project page goes through `cacheable_response`, which sets none.) And the pre-fix rewriter re-pointed every anchor it recognised at a local proxy path, so this did **not** give an upstream a way to steer pip to an off-registry host or to bypass the download-path gates — the breakout injects attributes, not a foreign href. The rewriter now emits a **fresh minimal PEP 503 document** built only from what it can vouch for, mirroring the #2967 ownership rebuild: one `<a>` per upstream anchor whose href yields a usable filename, its href rebuilt as `/pypi/{repo_key}/simple/{project}/{filename}` plus the upstream `#fragment`; the anchor's `data-*` attributes, entity-decoded and re-escaped into fresh double-quoted values (or emitted bare, as PEP 714 permits); and the escaped filename as the link text. Every other upstream byte is dropped, `<base>` among them, so `BASE_TAG_RE` and its fixpoint loop are gone — nothing survives for it to guard against. Absolute, root-relative and plain-relative upstream hrefs all keep routing through the proxy and no upstream host, path or query survives. **No client-visible information is lost:** `data-requires-python`, the PEP 658/714 `data-dist-info-metadata` / `data-core-metadata` attributes, `data-yanked` and the `#sha256=` fragment are all preserved, so version resolution, PEP 658 metadata fetching and hash checking are unaffected. One behaviour worth knowing: an anchor whose filename contains `<`, `>`, `"`, `'`, whitespace or a control character is **dropped rather than escaped and emitted** — such a "filename" is an attribute-breakout payload rather than a distribution name, the same posture `is_safe_upload_filename` takes at the ingest choke-point (#3107); no legal wheel or sdist filename contains those characters. The shared anchor and href matchers are now used by both this path and `filter_remote_case_a_html` rather than duplicated. **Am I affected:** only with a remote/proxy PyPI repository (or a virtual repository with a remote PyPI member), on the per-project route `/pypi/{repo}/simple/{project}/`; hosted repositories render their own index from database rows, and the `/simple/` root listing is rebuilt from normalized names, so neither was ever affected. On a public repository the page is served to unauthenticated callers. Note where the trust boundary sits — the payload has to come from the upstream index document, so the realistic actor is a hostile or compromised private mirror, corporate index or proxied registry, or someone able to tamper with that response; a publisher to public PyPI cannot reach this, because Warehouse escapes its own output and enforces a filename grammar excluding the necessary characters. **No cache purge is needed:** the proxy caches the raw upstream body and the page is rebuilt in the handler on every serve including warm cache hits, so a poisoned page already in the cache is sanitized on read as soon as this is deployed. **One route is deliberately not claimed as closed:** `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/download/simple/{project}/` on a Remote repository falls through to `proxy_fetch_streaming`, which streams the raw cached upstream body with the upstream's own content type (the `application/octet-stream` argument is only a fallback for when upstream sends none) and no rewriting. The same CSP ceiling applies, so the severity is unchanged rather than worse, but the class is not eliminated on that path.
- **npm, PyPI and Maven proxy-cache commits are gated on the upstream's published digest** (GHSA-qxv7-p3mq-88fv, #3347). Cargo (#2929) and OCI (#2274) already refused to *cache* a proxied body whose digest disagreed with the digest the upstream published for it. npm, PyPI and Maven each had that digest within reach and passed `None` to the gate, so the `dist.integrity` value the server itself served in a packument, the `#sha256=` fragment it served in a simple index and the `.sha1` sidecar it served beside a jar were decorative on the server side: a body that disagreed with them was committed to the proxy cache and served warm from then on. All three now resolve the digest and route through `fetch_artifact_streaming_with_cache_path_gated_digest`, a new algorithm-flexible sibling of the SHA-256-only gate — npm and Maven digests (SHA-512, SHA-1) are never observed by the storage layer, so the tee hashes the forwarded bytes for those algorithms. **npm** parses `dist.integrity` as SRI, preferring SHA-512 over SHA-256 over SHA-1 and skipping SHA-384 (no tee-hasher support), falling back to the legacy `dist.shasum`; the entry is matched to the request by the tarball URL's **filename**, never by parsing a version out of the requested filename (hyphenated names and prerelease tags make that ambiguous — the curation gate refuses to for the same reason, #2930), and the packument is re-read through the same cache-keyed helper the metadata handler uses, so an npm client that just fetched it finds it warm. **PyPI** lifts the `#sha256=` fragment from the same index page that vouched for the download URL; PEP 658 `.metadata` resolutions carry no fragment and stay ungated. **Maven** fetches the upstream `.sha1` sidecar, accepting the bare digest or the two-field `md5sum` form and only when the token is bare lowercase 40-hex, and gates **release versions only** — `-SNAPSHOT` is mutable and a racing re-deploy would pin a stale sidecar against a legitimate body, while checksum sidecars and `maven-metadata.xml` are excluded by `maven_proxy_package_name`'s own skip rules. Several properties matter more than the headline, and the one-line summary invites more confidence than the code earns. **The gate fails open:** no usable digest — absent, malformed, non-canonical (uppercase hex is rejected as unauthoritative and therefore silently ungated), an npm SRI carrying only the unsupported SHA-384, or an upstream error fetching the sidecar — and the fetch proceeds unverified exactly as before, so the protection is only as good as the upstream's digest publication. **A mismatch is served, not refused:** the bytes still reach the client, which verifies independently (npm checks the SRI, pip the hash, Maven and Gradle the sidecar), and are only kept out of the cache — this prevents persistent poisoning of the shared cache rather than protecting the requesting client, and a raw `curl` of a proxied artifact is unprotected. **How much an adversary is slowed differs by format:** for npm and Maven the digest travels from the *same upstream over the same channel* as the artifact — the packument, the adjacent `.sha1` — so an actor able to tamper with the body can tamper with the digest, and what these two reliably catch is divergence and corruption (a truncated body, a clean-EOF cut, a broken CDN edge); PyPI is meaningfully stronger, because the `#sha256=` comes from the index host while the file comes from the file host, a different origin on public PyPI, so it catches a misbehaving or compromised file host while the index is intact. That places these below OCI's pre-existing gate, whose expected digest is the *client-requested* blob digest rather than a value supplied by the server serving the bytes. One shared sharp edge: the expected digest is itself resolved cache-first, so a packument, index page or sidecar already poisoned in the cache supplies the attacker's digest and the gate admits the matching bytes. **Coverage inside the three formats is not total:** repositories with scan-on-proxy enabled take a buffered fetch path that is not gated (in the PyPI scanned path the resolved `expected_sha256` is carried and then never used), and npm and Maven **virtual** repositories resolve members through the ungated `fetch_artifact_streaming` entry point, though PyPI virtual member fetches are gated. The "mirroring the Cargo #2929 / OCI #2274 posture" claim is accurate — both are the same serve-but-don't-cache shape and both are likewise fail-open — but the set it joins is larger than the message says: **Debian** has had this since #2459, and its index is verified against a GPG-signed `Release`, making it the strongest of the group. So this brings the total to six gated formats out of roughly twenty-five proxied ones; NuGet, goproxy, Helm, Conda, Hex, RPM, Composer, Terraform, Git LFS and the rest still commit without a digest gate, several despite publishing a perfectly usable one. **Observability gap worth knowing:** a rejected commit emits a single `warn!` carrying only `cache_key` — no `target: "security"`, no `repository` field, no expected/actual digests, and **no metric**, so unlike the pre-existing content-addressed gate these events do not increment `ak_proxy_cache_checksum_mismatch_total` and will not reach an operator already alerting on it. **Am I affected:** only with remote/proxy npm, PyPI or Maven repositories. Upgrading admits no further unverified entries but evicts nothing already cached — see the upgrade note above.

## [1.7.3] - 2026-08-13

### Note — there is no 1.7.2 release

**1.7.2 was skipped and the version number is retired.** During the 1.7.2 cut the backend and openscap images were published to both registries before the release gate completed, and were publicly pullable for roughly nine hours before those tags were deleted. Republishing the same number with different content is precisely the "same tag, different bytes" hazard the exact-version immutability check exists to prevent, so the number was retired rather than re-pointed — everything staged for 1.7.2 ships here instead, unchanged in scope. **If you pulled a `1.7.2` image inside that window, discard it and take 1.7.3**: those bytes carry no release-gate certification, and while the tags no longer resolve in either registry, a local or mirrored cache can still hold a copy. No GitHub Release was ever published for 1.7.2, so a reader going from 1.7.1 to 1.7.3 has not missed one.

### Upgrade note — migration 193 blocks OCI writes while it runs, and the backend is not listening yet

`backend/migrations/193_oci_blobs_storage_key_index.sql` builds a plain `CREATE INDEX` on `oci_blobs`. Plan a write window before deploying, and on Kubernetes raise your startup/liveness thresholds first.

**The mechanism.** Migrations run at `main.rs:223`; the HTTP listener does not bind until `main.rs:1183`. For the whole index build the backend is therefore **not listening at all** — health probes get connection-refused, not a `503`, because there is no server to answer them. `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` is deliberately not used: `sqlx::migrate!` runs each migration file inside a transaction and Postgres rejects `CONCURRENTLY` there, the same constraint migrations 166 and 194 are under. A plain `CREATE INDEX` takes a `SHARE` lock, which conflicts with `ROW EXCLUSIVE`, so for the duration of the build **writes to `oci_blobs` block and reads do not**: blob commits on the push path wait, as do blob GC's row deletes, while plain `SELECT`s take `ACCESS SHARE` and are unaffected. `docker pull` keeps working while `docker push` hangs, and that asymmetry is the diagnostic signature — if pushes are hanging during a deploy while pulls are fine, this is what you are looking at. It is *not* `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`: nothing here rewrites the table, so plan the window against blocked writers, not blocked readers.

**The failure mode is a crash loop, not a slow start.** The migration runner raises its timeouts session-locally — `statement_timeout = '30min'` (`main.rs:217`) and `lock_timeout = '5min'` (`main.rs:220`). The lock timeout is the one that bites: if a long-running transaction holds a conflicting lock on `oci_blobs` for more than five minutes, the migration errors, `run_server` returns `Err`, and the process exits. Kubernetes restarts it, it takes the lock timeout again, and the deployment never comes up. Drain or kill long transactions touching `oci_blobs` before deploying.

**Kubernetes is the real risk.** A `livenessProbe` with a short `failureThreshold` kills the pod mid-`CREATE INDEX`. The transaction rolls back, the partially built index is discarded, and the replacement pod starts the build again from zero — on a large registry that never converges; it just spends a write stall per attempt, forever. Raise `startupProbe.failureThreshold` to cover the build (or the liveness thresholds, if you have no startup probe) **before** you deploy this release, not after the first pod is killed.

**No build-time estimate is given here, because none has been measured.** `oci_blobs` holds one row per stored blob and is much smaller than `artifacts`, so the build is expected to be short — but "expected" is not a number, and the only honest input is your own row count. Size it first, on the running 1.7.1 instance:

```sql
SELECT count(*) FROM oci_blobs;
```

**Avoiding the stall entirely.** Build the index out of band against the live table, before deploying:

```sql
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_oci_blobs_storage_key ON oci_blobs (storage_key);
```

The migration's `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` then finds it and is a no-op. This is the only supported way to avoid the stall — the migration cannot simply be skipped for one file, because sqlx tracks applied migrations by version and checksum and a missing entry blocks the ledger. Do build it, one way or the other: without the index the OCI cleanup sweep's per-candidate liveness re-check falls back to a sequential scan (measured on 300k blobs at 87 ms per hold versus 0.19 ms with it, ~163x over a full 1,000-row batch), and the cost scales linearly with registry size, so on a large registry a batch approaches the 15-minute claim TTL, leases lapse mid-sweep, and storage reclamation stops making progress. Skipping it trades a bounded write stall for an unbounded GC stall (#3085).

### Upgrade note — rolling back to 1.7.1 requires `SKIP_MIGRATIONS=true`

The 1.7.3 schema is forward-compatible with a 1.7.1 binary, but a 1.7.1 binary will **refuse to start** against a 1.7.3 database unless you set `SKIP_MIGRATIONS=true`. You set the flag that sounds dangerous in order to roll back safely; that inversion is exactly what nobody works out mid-incident, so it is written down here rather than left to be discovered.

**The data is fine.** 1.7.3 adds three migrations and all three are additive. 193 creates an index. 194 adds `oci_upload_cleanup_keys.pending_delete_at` (`TIMESTAMPTZ`, nullable, no default) plus a partial index over it. 195 adds six columns to `curation_packages`: `attestation_state TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unverified'` and five nullable ones. Nothing is dropped, renamed or retyped, and every added column either carries a safe default or accepts `NULL`, so a 1.7.1 binary that never mentions them still writes correct rows. There is no data migration to undo and no restore-from-backup step.

**What blocks the rollback is the migration ledger, not the data.** `main.rs:223` runs `sqlx::migrate!("./migrations")`, whose `Migrator` carries `ignore_missing = false`. On startup sqlx validates the applied set recorded in `_sqlx_migrations` against the versions embedded in the binary and returns `MigrateError::VersionMissing` for any applied version the binary does not know about. v1.7.1 embeds migrations up to 192; a 1.7.3 database has 193, 194 and 195 applied. The 1.7.1 binary therefore fails validation and exits before it binds — the same symptom as the crash loop above, from an entirely different cause, which is why the two notes sit together.

**Am I affected?** Only if you roll back. The supported path is to set `SKIP_MIGRATIONS=true` on the 1.7.1 deployment: it takes the `if skip_migrations` branch at `main.rs:203-205`, logs `SKIP_MIGRATIONS=true, skipping automatic database migrations`, and never constructs the `Migrator` at all, so nothing validates the ledger. The one-shot legacy repair routines that run ahead of the gate (`main.rs:191-196`) still run and still no-op on a healthy database. Rolling forward again is simply removing the flag — 193 through 195 are already applied, so the runner has nothing to do. Do **not** delete rows from `_sqlx_migrations` as an alternative: sqlx will then try to re-run those files against a schema that already contains their objects.

### Upgrade note — authenticated callers now need a grant to pull private OCI/Docker repositories

OCI `/v2` reads are authorized. Previously the only check on the pull path was whether the caller was *anonymous*, so **any authenticated principal could pull any private image** by name or digest, and `GET /v2/<name>/tags/list` handed a private repository's tag names to the same audience. Writes were never affected.

**The mechanism.** `/v2` is nested in the router ahead of `format_routes`, and `repo_visibility_middleware` — the layer that authorizes every other format's reads — is attached to `format_routes` only, so the entire OCI read surface sat outside it. Inside the handlers the guard was `if is_anon && !repo.is_public`: `authenticate_oci` establishes *who* the caller is and never asked whether they may read this repository. The neighbouring gates did not close it either — `enforce_token_repo_scope` is unrestricted for a JWT session, `require_scope` is satisfied unconditionally by a principal carrying `scopes: None`, and `can_access_repo` is a token-scope ceiling (`AccessScope::Admin => true`), never a visibility check. All five read verbs — `GET`/`HEAD` blob, `GET`/`HEAD` manifest and `GET tags/list` — now run `require_oci_repo_read_access`, which asks `PermissionService::check_repository_action`, the same choke point the OCI write gate already used. That is deliberately not a new predicate: a role carrying `admin` (the `repository-owner` row repository creation seeds) always wins, an applicable fine-grained rule stays authoritative for the principals it names, and every other principal keeps its role-based capabilities. A denial is `403 DENIED` when fine-grained rules govern the repository and `404 NAME_UNKNOWN` when none do, with a body byte-identical to the one a genuinely missing repository returns, so the two answers cannot be used to probe which private repositories exist (#3261).

**Am I affected?** Yes, if any principal pulls from a private OCI/Docker repository while holding no role assignment, no fine-grained `read`/`admin` rule and no repository ownership — a CI service account carrying only an API token and no grant is the usual case. Give those principals a grant before upgrading. Anonymous pulls, public repositories, admin pulls, repository owners and every already-granted principal are unaffected, and the `401` + `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer` challenge that drives the `docker login` handshake is untouched. Vulnerability scanning of private images also keeps working: `_ak_scanner` is seeded with no grants by design, so the single-repository token `generate_scan_token` mints is accepted as the authorization it is, after `enforce_scan_pull_scope` has already confirmed the token names this repository.

**Finding those principals before you upgrade.** "Give them a grant" is only actionable if you can list them. This is the inverse of `PermissionService::check_repository_action(user, repo, 'read', false)` — the same admin-role arm, the same rules-are-authoritative-when-present `CASE`, the same group and project arms — evaluated over everyone who has actually pulled from a private OCI-backed repository, so it returns principals that will start being denied rather than every ungranted account in the instance:

```sql
SELECT u.username, r.key AS repository, max(ds.downloaded_at) AS last_pull
FROM download_statistics ds
JOIN artifacts a    ON a.id = ds.artifact_id
JOIN repositories r ON r.id = a.repository_id
JOIN users u        ON u.id = ds.user_id
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (
  SELECT
    EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM role_assignments ra JOIN roles ro ON ro.id = ra.role_id
             WHERE ra.user_id = u.id
               AND (ra.repository_id = r.id OR ra.repository_id IS NULL)
               AND 'admin' = ANY(ro.permissions))                       AS role_admin,
    EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM permissions p
             WHERE ((p.principal_type IN ('user','service_account') AND p.principal_id = u.id)
                 OR (p.principal_type = 'group' AND p.principal_id IN
                      (SELECT group_id FROM user_group_members WHERE user_id = u.id)))
               AND ((p.target_type = 'repository' AND p.target_id = r.id)
                 OR (p.target_type = 'project'    AND p.target_id = r.project_id))) AS has_rule,
    EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM permissions p
             WHERE ((p.principal_type IN ('user','service_account') AND p.principal_id = u.id)
                 OR (p.principal_type = 'group' AND p.principal_id IN
                      (SELECT group_id FROM user_group_members WHERE user_id = u.id)))
               AND ((p.target_type = 'repository' AND p.target_id = r.id)
                 OR (p.target_type = 'project'    AND p.target_id = r.project_id))
               AND ('read' = ANY(p.actions) OR 'admin' = ANY(p.actions)))           AS rule_grants,
    EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM role_assignments ra JOIN roles ro ON ro.id = ra.role_id
             WHERE ra.user_id = u.id
               AND (ra.repository_id = r.id OR ra.repository_id IS NULL)
               AND ('read' = ANY(ro.permissions) OR 'admin' = ANY(ro.permissions))) AS role_grants
) g
WHERE r.format::text IN ('docker','podman','buildx','oras','wasm_oci','helm_oci')
  AND r.is_public = false
  AND u.is_admin = false
  AND ds.downloaded_at > NOW() - INTERVAL '90 days'
  AND NOT (g.role_admin OR CASE WHEN g.has_rule THEN g.rule_grants ELSE g.role_grants END)
GROUP BY 1, 2
ORDER BY 3 DESC;
```

Read it as a floor, not a census: `download_statistics` records a row for a manifest `GET` and not for a `HEAD`, `user_id` is `NULL` for anonymous pulls, and a service account that has not pulled inside the window will not appear. Widen the interval, or drop the `downloaded_at` predicate entirely, if your CI fleet pulls seasonally.

**If you miss one, the symptom will not look like an authorization failure.** The `403 DENIED` arm only fires when the repository carries rows in the `permissions` table. Grants usually come from `role_assignments` instead, so the common denial is the other arm: `404 NAME_UNKNOWN`, with a body byte-identical to the one a genuinely missing repository returns. `docker login` still succeeds, because the token handshake is untouched; `GET /v2/<name>/tags/list` 404s the same way; and there is **no server-side log line on a denial** — only the lookup-failure paths log. The on-call engineer's first hypothesis will therefore be "GC deleted our images", and Docker lifecycle retention *also* changed in this release (#2998, #3026, under Fixed), so that hypothesis will look plausible for longer than it should. If images vanish for one principal and not another after this upgrade, check grants before you check retention.

**Known limitation, and what it costs (#3269).** `GET /v2/_catalog` still returns every repository key present in `oci_tags` to any authenticated caller, with no per-repository filtering — name enumeration, not content. It is left as-is because the Docker Registry HTTP API V2 makes catalog contents implementation-specific and most registries behave the same way, and it is targeted at 1.8.0. **Read it together with the `404` denial above, because the two interact.** The `404 NAME_UNKNOWN` arm was deliberately made byte-identical to a genuinely missing repository so that a denial could not be used to probe which private repositories exist. That property is real at the `/v2/<name>` seam and worth keeping — but it protects nothing an authenticated caller cannot obtain in a single request to `/v2/_catalog`. So the accurate summary of this release is narrower than "private OCI repositories are now protected": private image *content* is protected, and the repository *namespace* is not. Treat every repository key as visible to every authenticated principal until #3269 lands, and do not rely on an unguessable repository name as a layer of defence.

### Upgrade note — backups on filesystem storage start reporting `failed`, and your existing archives never held artifact bytes

On a deployment using the filesystem storage backend — the default — `POST /api/v1/admin/backups/{id}/execute` now answers `200` with `status: "failed"` and an `error_message` naming artifact objects it could not read, on any instance holding at least one artifact. In 1.7.1 the same call reported `completed` with `artifact_count=0`. **Nothing about what the archive contains has changed.** These archives did not contain artifact bytes before this release and do not contain them now. This is the first release in which the deployment tells you so, which is the whole of the change and also the reason it reads like a regression when it is not one.

**The mechanism.** Two things compose. The first is #2863, open and pre-existing: a filesystem repository is rooted at its own directory — `storage_path` is `{STORAGE_PATH}/{repository key}` — and the format handlers write objects relative to that root, so an object recorded as `storage_key = a1/36/a136b2c2…` physically lives at `{STORAGE_PATH}/{repository key}/a1/36/a136b2c2…`. The backup path reads `artifacts.storage_key` through the deployment-wide storage handle and therefore looks for it at `{STORAGE_PATH}/a1/36/a136b2c2…`, which is nothing. Not some artifacts: on a filesystem deployment *every* artifact read misses, and the database export in `database/*.json` is all the archive has ever held. The second is #3170, in this release: that read failure used to be discarded by an `if let Ok(content)`, so the job recorded `completed` over an archive with no content in it — a backup that reports success while holding nothing is the worst failure a backup can have, because it is discovered at restore time when there is no recourse. Read failures are now logged, counted into the manifest's `artifacts_unreadable`, and fail the job. #3327 then moved that failure to where an operator can see it: the trigger endpoint returned a bare `500 {"code":"INTERNAL_ERROR","message":"Internal server error"}`, discarding at the HTTP boundary the very message that names the missing objects, and now answers with the backup resource carrying `status` and `error_message`.

**Am I affected?** Yes, if `STORAGE_BACKEND` is unset or `filesystem` and the instance holds any artifact rows. An empty instance still reports `completed` — it has nothing to miss, which is why a fresh test namespace looks healthy and a real one does not. Object-storage deployments are **not** affected: S3, GCS and Azure resolve to one shared namespace for the whole deployment, so the recorded `storage_key` is the whole key and the read finds the object. A mixed deployment splits the same way, per repository: repositories assigned to a named cloud backend are captured, repositories on filesystem are not. Scheduled backups are affected identically to manual ones: the scheduler still takes the failed-run path, so the occurrence is finalized as failed in `backup_schedule_runs` and the backup metric records a failure — a behaviour change for anything alerting on either, and one that will fire on the first scheduled run after the upgrade rather than at the next restore.

**Reading an archive you already have.** The manifest is a plain file in the tarball, so an existing archive can be audited without restoring it:

```
tar -xzOf backup.tar.gz manifest.json | jq '{artifact_count, artifacts_unreadable, database_tables}'
```

`artifacts_unreadable` does not exist in an archive written before 1.7.3 and deserializes as `0`, so it cannot confirm anything about an old archive — **the tell in a pre-1.7.3 archive is `artifact_count: 0` on an instance that was not empty.** The `database_tables` list is genuine: `users`, `repositories`, `artifacts`, `download_statistics`, `api_tokens`, `roles`, `user_roles` and `permission_grants` were exported and are restorable. What you have is a metadata backup. Plan your disaster recovery on that basis until #2863 lands, and keep an independent copy of `{STORAGE_PATH}` — a filesystem-level snapshot or an `rsync` of that tree is what actually holds your artifact bytes today.

**Object storage is not a workaround for an instance that already exists.** Changing `STORAGE_BACKEND` does not move a single byte, and there is no supported in-place migration that re-lays a per-repository filesystem tree into the shared object namespace and reconciles `artifacts.storage_key` to match. Existing repositories keep the `storage_backend` recorded on their row. Object storage is the right answer for a deployment being stood up now, and no answer at all for one already running on filesystem.

**What `allow_partial_artifacts` buys, and what it does not.** Setting `allow_partial_artifacts: true` in a backup's `metadata` makes the run report `completed` instead of `failed`, keeps the miss count in the manifest, and still makes a restore of that archive record an error rather than reporting `errors=[]`. It **does not put a single byte into the archive** — it is an acknowledgement, not a repair, and an archive produced under it is exactly as empty of artifact content as one produced without it. Reach for it only if a `failed` job is breaking automation you cannot change quickly, and do not let `completed` be read as "the backup is fine". Note also that the flag has no API surface in this release: `POST /admin/backups` does not accept it and there is no update endpoint, so the only way to set it is an `UPDATE backups SET metadata = metadata || '{"allow_partial_artifacts":true}'::jsonb WHERE id = …` between creating the backup and executing it.

**Tracking.** #2863 is the root-cause fix — the read path, the archive's flat key namespace, and the restore side all have to move together, so it is milestoned 1.8.0 with an assessment recorded on the issue. Separately, OCI blobs live in `oci_blobs` rather than `artifacts` and are never enumerated by the backup at all, so they are absent from every archive on every backend for an independent reason.

### Upgrade note — two OCI `/v2` error paths now return the spec error envelope

A client that parses the **body** of an OCI scope denial or a curation block sees a different shape after upgrading. The status codes are unchanged; only the bodies are.

**The mechanism.** Two `/v2` error paths did not emit the `{"errors":[{"code":…,"message":…,"detail":…}]}` document the OCI distribution spec requires of a JSON 4XX body. `oci_forbidden_scope` — the API-token scope gate on the monolithic blob `POST`, upload `PATCH`/`PUT`, blob `DELETE`, manifest `PUT` and manifest `DELETE` — returned a bare string, `Token does not have required scope: …`; that text is now preserved verbatim inside an envelope with code `DENIED`. The shared curation gate reached from manifest `GET`/`HEAD` leaked the REST-shaped `{"error":"curation_blocked","package":…,"reason":…}` body onto the OCI wire, which is a differently-shaped JSON object and therefore a spec violation; it now renders `DENIED` with the `curation_blocked` marker kept in `message` **and the blocking rule's reason carried in both `message` and `detail`**, which an operator debugging a blocked `docker pull` previously did not get at all. The two fallible extractors on `/v2/token` (a duplicated `service` query parameter, and a request body over the token-endpoint limit) were the last bare-string 4XX bodies reachable on this router and are re-shaped the same way, keeping their original status and text under code `UNSUPPORTED`. Every non-OCI format's curation body is byte-identical to before, pinned by a test — the seam was split so the shared gate returns a structured verdict and each protocol renders it (#3110).

**Am I affected?** Only if something in your tooling reads those bodies rather than the status code. `docker`, `podman` and other spec-conformant clients are unaffected — they already parse the envelope. The companion change lands in `artifact-keeper-test`, where the curation DTF oracle selected on `.error` by field lookup and would have gone red on the first release carrying this fix; it now requires the envelope at ≥ 1.7.2 and accepts either shape below it. That floor names 1.7.2 deliberately even though no 1.7.2 shipped: the oracle compares with `>=`, so this release is held to the envelope, and keeping the floor one number lower also holds the `dev` images built from `main` while the workspace version read 1.7.2 — which already carried this fix.

### Upgrade note — the fine-grained permissions API is admin-only

`GET /api/v1/permissions`, `GET /api/v1/permissions/{id}` and `DELETE /api/v1/permissions/{id}` now require admin. Non-admin API consumers of those three endpoints will start receiving `403`.

**The mechanism.** The write endpoints were admin-gated, the reads and the delete were not. The two read handlers extracted no `AuthExtension` at all, so any authenticated principal — including a read-scoped service-account token — could page the whole ACL table and learn which principals hold `admin` on which repositories. `DELETE` checked only `require_scope("delete")`, and `AuthExtension::has_scope` returns `true` unconditionally when `scopes` is `None`, which is what every interactive JWT session carries — so any logged-in non-admin could delete arbitrary permission rows. All three now use the same `require_auth` + scope + `require_admin()` shape the create and update handlers already had, before any database work (#3229).

**Am I affected?** Only if a non-admin principal calls those three endpoints. The shipped consumers are all admin surfaces — the web UI reads the list only from its admin route group, and the CLI `permission` command is an admin management command — so no shipped workflow breaks. Admin-only was chosen over a filtered "grants naming me" view because the endpoint has no per-caller filtering model today; a self-service view should be a deliberately designed endpoint rather than a widening of this one.

### Upgrade note — eight smaller operational changes worth two minutes before you deploy

Each of these is documented in full under Security, Changed or Fixed. Collected here is only the part an operator has to act on, ordered by how likely it is to page someone who cannot immediately tell what happened.

- **Storage alerts will fire on upgrade day, and it is not an incident** (#3134, #3249). `total_storage_bytes` now sums the `repository_usage_ledger` instead of aggregating `artifacts`, so on a Docker-heavy registry the reported total steps up by the entire OCI blob volume — layers were previously invisible because only manifests land in `artifacts` — and proxy-cached bytes fold in as well. The daily `storage_metrics` snapshot changes basis on the same day, so growth charts show a one-day vertical step. **Any alert on `total_storage_bytes` or on storage growth rate will fire, and it will look like a real incident.** It is display-only: quota admission reads the per-repository `repository_usage_ledger` row and is unchanged, so **no repository becomes over-quota as a result of this**. Record the pre-upgrade values, silence or re-baseline storage-growth alerts for 48 hours, and annotate the series at the cut. Historical `storage_metrics` rows are deliberately not backfilled — the source tables hold no history, so a backfill could only fabricate one.
- **Curation freshness silently dropped 12x — in 1.7.1, not in this release, so if you are on 1.7.1 you are already affected** (#2219). This one is not an upgrade action; it is a *check now* action, and it is listed here because 1.7.1's published notes did not carry it. `curation_sync_interval_secs` was read into the row and never used, so every enabled staging repository re-synced on every 5-minute scheduler tick. **1.7.1 (2026-08-07) started honouring it** (`scheduler_service.rs:1518-1533` on this release, 60-second floor; migration 190 added the `curation_last_synced_at` stamp), and the column default is **3600** (`migrations/071_curation.sql:54`). So unless you explicitly set the field, curation freshness went from roughly 5 minutes to 1 hour **the moment you deployed 1.7.1** — it does not change again at 1.7.3. The failure signature is "a package we blocked upstream an hour ago is still installable", and there is no metric, no log line and no alert for it, which is why an instance can sit at the new interval for weeks without anyone noticing. Run this now if you are on 1.7.1, rather than at your next upgrade window, and lower the interval where an hour is too slow (`curation_last_synced_at` exists only on 1.7.1 and later; on 1.7.0 or below, drop it from the projection):
  ```sql
  SELECT id, curation_sync_interval_secs, curation_last_synced_at
  FROM repositories WHERE curation_enabled;
  ```
- **PyPI project names that were never valid become permanently unreachable** (#3186, #3198). Read routes now reject a `:project` segment that fails the PEP 508 name pattern with `404` before any lookup, and uploads reject the same with `400`. Malformed names were previously coerced rather than validated, and some of them were *stored* — an artifact whose project name is empty or otherwise outside the pattern still consumes quota but is now reachable at no URL, with no migration to repair it. This is the correct fix and it is a silent data-visibility loss, so inventory before you upgrade rather than after. The predicate is the enforced regex itself (`formats::pypi_name::PEP508_NAME_RE`), written in the spec's `^`/`$` spelling:
  ```sql
  SELECT r.key AS repository, a.name AS project, count(*) AS artifacts
  FROM artifacts a
  JOIN repositories r ON r.id = a.repository_id
  WHERE r.format::text IN ('pypi','poetry','conda')
    AND a.is_deleted = false
    AND a.name !~ '^([A-Za-z0-9]|[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]*[A-Za-z0-9])$'
  GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;
  ```
  Zero rows means nothing becomes unreachable. Rows returned are what to re-publish under a valid name (or delete) before upgrading.
- **The scan-gate widening only bites deployments that configured a policy** (#3220, #3143, #3142). `PolicyService::evaluate_artifact` short-circuits to allowed when no enabled policy matches (`policy_service.rs:152-173`), and nothing seeds a default row, so one query sizes the entire exposure:
  ```sql
  SELECT id, name, repository_id, is_enabled, block_unscanned, block_on_fail, max_severity
  FROM scan_policies;
  ```
  Zero rows means zero risk from all three changes. If rows come back, two amplifiers are worth knowing: a row with `repository_id IS NULL` is **global** and applies to every repository, and since migration 121 a policy created without an explicit `block_unscanned` gets `true`.
- **An OpenSCAP `critical` rule failure now costs 25 security-score points, where it cost 0** (#3294). The OpenSCAP adapter had no `critical` arm and filed such a result at `info`, which is inert: it carries a penalty weight of `0`, belongs to no `policy_service` severity array, and is selected by no gate's count projection. It now classifies as `critical`, and `recalculate_score` — which runs automatically after every scan and counts one row per `(artifact, scan_type)`, so OpenSCAP competes alongside Trivy and Grype rather than replacing them — charges 25 points each. On an otherwise-clean repository **1 such finding takes it from grade A to B, 2 to C, 3 to D, and 4 to F**. The per-finding effect is also broader than a re-grade: `critical` is a member of *every* `policy_service` severity array, so a single one violates a scan policy at **any** `max_severity`, not just the permissive settings — where as `info` it violated none. That reaches the artifact download gate (`403`), both promotion CVE gates, the approval gate, and any `min_health_score` or `min_security_score` quality gate. **Am I affected?** Only if you run OpenSCAP — it is opt-in and registers only when `OPENSCAP_URL` is set — *and* your SCAP content declares `severity="critical"` on a rule. XCCDF 1.2 defines the attribute as `unknown | info | low | medium | high` (NISTIR 7275r4 §6.4.4.2), so `critical` is outside the standard vocabulary and appears only in custom or vendor-extended content; Artifact Keeper's wrapper passes through whatever the rule declares. Stock SSG content is unaffected. Nothing is backfilled — stored `scan_findings` rows keep their recorded `info` and a repository moves only when its artifacts are next scanned — so the change lands one repository at a time and can be staged. Findings that are acknowledged are excluded from the score. To size it before upgrading:
  ```sql
  SELECT r.key AS repository, count(*) AS findings
  FROM scan_findings f
  JOIN scan_results s ON s.id = f.scan_result_id
  JOIN artifacts a ON a.id = s.artifact_id
  JOIN repositories r ON r.id = a.repository_id
  WHERE s.scan_type = 'openscap' AND f.severity = 'info' AND NOT f.is_acknowledged
  GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 2 DESC;
  ```
  Rows returned are the repositories where a re-scan can move the grade; how far depends on how many of those findings the content grades `critical`. This is a fail-closed correction — a `critical` compliance failure filed at the floor meant a severity gate silently not firing — but it is not a silent one, which is why it is here.
- **`helm push oci://` into a classic Helm repository now returns `400`, and charts pushed that way disappear from the index** (#3150). Legacy OCI manifest rows are excluded from `index.yaml` generation (`helm.rs:119-134`, `is_oci_manifest_artifact`), so the operator-visible event is "our charts vanished from `helm repo update` and our publish job started failing". The index those rows produced was never valid, and reads are deliberately left open so the bytes can be pulled back out, but the disappearance is abrupt. Re-publish affected charts to a `helm_oci` repository, or push them as classic charts.
- **The OCI push path can now answer `503`, on a path that never did before** (#3187). When a push races the cleanup sweep reclaiming the same storage key, the monolithic blob `POST` (`oci_v2.rs:5497`) and the chunked completion (`oci_v2.rs:6962`) return `503` with code `BLOB_UPLOAD_INVALID`, the message `blob storage was being reclaimed concurrently; retry the upload`, and `Retry-After: 1`. The window is narrow — it needs a digest whose orphaned blob object is at least 24 hours old, unreferenced, and actively being reclaimed — but `BLOB_UPLOAD_INVALID` reads as terminal to some clients, so a CI pipeline with no push retry will see intermittent, unexplained failures. Add a retry around `docker push` if you do not have one.
