- **The S3 bulk request timeout default moves from 30s to 1800s** (#3180). The knobs themselves are listed under Added; the default change is the operational item. A wedged S3 endpoint previously failed a bulk request in 30 seconds; it can now hold the connection for up to 30 minutes, which turns a fast fail into connection-pool and task pileup during a partial object-store brownout. Control-plane calls (head, exists, list, delete, multipart create/abort) keep the short 30-second cliff, so this applies only to payload movement — object reads and writes, multipart parts, and server-side `CopyObject`. If you sized your pool around a 30-second worst case, set `S3_BULK_TIMEOUT_SECS` deliberately rather than inheriting the new default.

### Security

- **A repository-scoped API token presented directly to `/v2/` is now confined to its repositories, for reads and writes** (#3316). A token minted with an `allowed_repo_ids` allow-list is the standard containment primitive for CI service accounts and registry mirrors. That ceiling — `enforce_token_repo_scope`, which #3261's upgrade note describes as running "as the first act of the read gate ahead of the admin bypass" — was only ever populated for tokens laundered through the `/v2/token` exchange. On the **direct-credential** path — the way `docker login`, `-u user:token` and a raw `Authorization: Bearer <token>` actually present a credential — `authenticate_oci_with_scopes` re-minted its claims through `generate_tokens(&user)`, which sets `allowed_repo_ids: None` (unrestricted), and the scope allow-list was silently discarded. A token scoped to repository A could therefore **read and write every other repository the owning identity holds any grant on**: `tags/list`, manifest and blob `GET`/`HEAD` all returned `200`, and a blob upload followed by a manifest `PUT` created tags in a repository the token was never scoped to. Only the repository ceiling was lost — the action-scope ceiling (read-only vs. write) travels through a separate channel and still bit, so a read-only token could not write even to its own repository. **Am I affected:** every prior version, on any deployment that issues repository-scoped API tokens and lets them reach `/v2/` directly (i.e. normal Docker/OCI usage — the token is sent as the Basic password or a Bearer, without an intervening `/v2/token` exchange). A token laundered through `/v2/token` was already confined; the direct path was not. To reason about exposure, treat any repository-scoped token that has been used against `/v2/` as if it held the owning identity's full read+write reach over the registry for the period it was valid. The fix threads the validated ceiling onto the minted claims on both direct-credential branches (Bearer slot and Basic password slot), mirroring the exchange path; a token whose allow-list resolves to nothing (`Restricted(vec![])`) maps to a deny-all, not to unrestricted. A JWT that was itself exchanged from a scoped token and then presented as the Docker password is now confined too — that arm re-minted and would otherwise have laundered the ceiling away, a bypass of this same fix. Only the repository ceiling is threaded here; the token's read-only-vs-write action ceiling was never lost and is unchanged. **This is a fail-closed behaviour change:** a CI pipeline whose scoped token has been reaching repositories outside its declared scope will begin receiving a denial on those repositories after upgrading. That is the intended outcome — the token is now doing what its scope always said — but scope any token to the repositories it genuinely needs before rolling this out, or the first failed pull/push after upgrade will be the notification. The denial shape depends on the repository kind: a direct (Local/Remote) repository outside scope answers `403 DENIED`, while a **Virtual** repository answers `404` — a token scoped to a virtual `V` clears the parent gate but each member is then re-checked against the token's scope (`member_passes_token_scope`) and dropped, so the parent resolves as if empty rather than returning an explicit 403. Both are refusals; only the status code differs.
- **A public Virtual repository no longer serves a private member's bytes to anonymous callers over the Helm, NuGet V2 and Swift download routes** (#3324). The route middleware admits an unauthenticated caller whenever the URL repository is public, and then authorizes only that repository — never the members a Virtual repository resolves through. Three download paths walked the member set unfiltered from there, each streaming a **private** member's stored artifact to an anonymous caller through a public virtual parent: `GET /helm/{repo}/charts/{filename}` served a hosted member's chart `.tgz` (and `.prov`) via `download_chart_via_index`, `GET /nuget/{repo}/v2/package/{id}/{version}` — the legacy V2/Chocolatey protocol — served a member's `.nupkg` while the V3 flat-container sibling was already authorized, and `GET /swift/{repo}/{scope}/{name}/{version}/Package.swift` served a member's manifest source while Swift's own archive download was already authorized. All three now thread the caller through and narrow the member walk with the same `proxy_helpers::authorize_virtual_members` predicate the OCI virtual walkers use — a member contributes bytes when it is public or the caller holds a grant on it and it passes the token-scope ceiling — rather than inventing a new predicate. A denied member reads as an ordinary miss, so neither the bytes nor the member's existence leak; a *failed* visibility query surfaces as a retryable server error rather than being flattened into a definitive "not found". Anonymous downloads of **public** members' content through the same virtual repositories are unchanged, as are authorized callers. **Am I affected?** Yes, if you publish a public Virtual Helm, NuGet or Swift repository that aggregates private members and rely on those members' contents staying private from unauthenticated callers; the members' direct routes were always gated and writes were never affected. Note the scope of what this closes: virtual-repository laundering is now shut for OCI (#3261, #3320) and for these three **byte** paths — metadata-level exposure through virtual parents (index documents, feeds and version listings that may still enumerate private members' package names and versions, including Helm's merged `index.yaml` and the NuGet OData feed) is a distinct, lower-severity gap tracked in #3323 and is **not** closed by this release.
- **OCI/Docker `/v2` reads are authorized rather than merely checked for anonymity** (#3261). See the upgrade note above for the operator-facing change. Two further gaps were closed alongside the main one: a **public Virtual repository laundered a private member's bytes** — `resolve_virtual_manifest` and `resolve_virtual_blob` walked the member set unfiltered, so an anonymous caller could receive a private member's manifest and layers through a public virtual parent. Both now filter members through `proxy_helpers::authorize_virtual_members`, the same helper Maven, npm, PyPI, Debian and goproxy use, and a denied member is simply dropped from the walk so member existence is not leaked either. Because `VirtualResolveKey` carries no principal term, the negative resolve cache is consulted and populated **only** when the caller can see every member — otherwise a denied caller's miss would be replayed to a granted one as a false "not found", turning the authorization filter into a cache-poisoning primitive. Second, the token repo-scope ceiling and the scoped-admin demotion now run as the first act of the read gate, ahead of the admin and scanner bypasses, matching the write gate — and `handle_tags_list` reaches that same gate, so all five read verbs run the ceiling through one path rather than only the manifest and blob handlers that also call it explicitly. One residual remains, filed as #3269: `GET /v2/_catalog` still lists every repository key to any authenticated caller (name enumeration, not content). See the upgrade note for why that residual constrains what the rest of this change buys you.
- **A public Virtual OCI repository no longer leaks a private member's tag names** (#3320). The #3261 work filtered `resolve_virtual_manifest` and `resolve_virtual_blob` through `proxy_helpers::authorize_virtual_members`, but the third virtual walker — `tags_list_virtual`, behind `GET /v2/<name>/tags/list` — was missed and still merged every member's tags unfiltered. Combined with the read gate's `if is_anon && !repo.is_public` short-circuit (an anonymous caller on a *public* parent never reaches the gate at all), an unauthenticated `GET /v2/<public-virtual>/tags/list` returned the tag names of the virtual's **private** members. Tag/repository names only, not artifact bytes — a metadata disclosure lower in severity than the manifest/blob laundering, but it contradicted the operator-facing claim above that OCI virtual laundering was closed. `tags_list_virtual` now threads the caller through and narrows the member set through the same `authorize_virtual_members` helper the manifest and blob walkers use: a member contributes its tags when it is public or the caller holds *any* grant on it (a role assignment or any fine-grained rule, regardless of the actions it carries) and it passes the token-scope ceiling, so anonymous and ungranted callers see public members only. Note the shared predicate is deliberately reused rather than a new one invented, and it is coarser than the direct read gate, which requires the `read` action — a principal holding only a write-scoped rule on a private member is refused a direct tags/list of it but still sees its tags through the virtual; that predicate gap is shared with the manifest/blob walkers and repository listing and is tracked separately. A denied member contributes nothing, which reads as "no tags there" and leaks neither tag names nor member existence, and a failed visibility query surfaces as a retryable server error rather than being flattened into the empty set (which the tags path would otherwise answer with a definitive `404 NAME_UNKNOWN`). An anonymous caller on a public virtual still sees the tags of its *public* members, so legitimate public pulls are unchanged. **Am I affected?** Yes, if you publish a Virtual OCI/Docker repository that aggregates private members and rely on those members' contents (including their tag names) staying private from unauthenticated callers. The private members' own direct read paths were already gated by #3261 and were never affected; writes were never affected.
- **Quarantine status and SBOM listings are gated on repository visibility, not token scope** (#3174). `can_access_repo` answers "is this repository within my token's scope?" — it resolves to `access_scope().grants(repo_id)` and `AccessScope::Admin => true` unconditionally, which is the shape of every browser JWT session, every unscoped API token and every global admin. Used as a visibility gate it returned `true` for every repository in the instance. `GET /api/v1/quarantine/{artifact_id}` therefore told any authenticated caller holding an artifact UUID from another tenant's **private** repository that the artifact exists, that it is held, and the free-text `quarantine_reason` — which carries the malware or CVE finding behind the hold. `GET /api/v1/sbom?repository_id=…` had the same single check in front of a private repository's whole dependency inventory. Both now use the canonical `is_public OR (in_scope AND (is_admin OR grants))` predicate. The reason text is deliberately gated **more** strictly than the existence check: it drops the `is_public` arm, so a caller who can see the artifact only because its repository happens to be public does not get the finding — which is what the pre-existing comment claimed and did not implement. A missing repository row now collapses into the same existence-hiding `404` instead of a message naming the key.
- **Virtual repositories no longer disclose members the caller cannot see** (#3163, #3081, #3177, #3178) — the same `can_access_repo`-as-visibility confusion, in five more places. `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/members` let any authenticated user who could reach a virtual repo enumerate the `key`, `name` and `repo_type` of every member including private repositories they hold no grant on, and the parent itself was never `require_visible`d, so a *private* virtual repository's membership was readable by anyone while `GET /{key}` on it correctly 404'd. `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}/artifacts` on a virtual repo aggregated private members' artifacts — and, in the `group_by=maven_component` view, their Maven GAV coordinates — straight into the response. A virtual repository's `storage_used_bytes` was summed over every member reachable through the membership graph, so a user granted the parent but not its members learned those members' exact byte sizes. And byte resolution itself was open: `proxy_helpers::resolve_virtual_download` took no caller at all, so it structurally could not filter, while the one predicate that did exist gated on `can_access_repo` and then treated `Ok(false)` as *allow*, making a private member carrying no fine-grained rules readable by any authenticated caller. Every one of these now evaluates the same `build_visibility_clause_for` composition the repository listing uses — public ∪ `role_assignments` ∪ fine-grained `permissions`, including the group and project arms — reusing one predicate rather than inventing a fourth. A member is readable through a virtual exactly when its own `GET` would succeed; denials collapse to the `404` a genuine miss produces, so "this virtual is empty" and "this virtual has members you may not see" are no longer distinguishable; the member walk fails **closed** on a database error; and `RepoVisibility::All` renders a literal `true`, so admin callers see byte-identical figures to before.
- **A write-only grant no longer buys read access to a private virtual member** (#3325). **This is a fail-closed change: a principal that has been relying on write-only-grant read access through a virtual repository will lose it.** The member filter added above admits a member on the existence of *any* grant — `permissions_grant_exists_for` tests only `actions <> '{}'`, and the `role_assignments` arm never joins `roles` — while a direct read of that same member requires the `read` action through `PermissionService::check_repository_action`. So a principal holding exactly one rule on a private member, `actions = {write}`, was refused `GET /v2/M/tags/list` and then handed that member's **manifest and blob bytes** through any virtual repository containing it. Because `authorize_virtual_members` is the single choke point for virtual byte resolution, the same gap was reachable through Maven, npm, PyPI, Debian, Go and the ~28 formats that route through `resolve_virtual_download`, not only OCI. The fix layers the canonical action choke-point on top of the existing grant filter — the identical `check_repository_action` call, with the identical arguments, that the direct `/v2` gate and the native-format middleware make — so a member is now readable through a virtual exactly when a direct read of it would succeed, by construction rather than by assertion. The gate sits in the shared `try_authorize_virtual_members` body rather than the `authorize_virtual_members` wrapper, so the aggregating tags/list path added in the same release inherits it; two regression tests pin that placement, one at the unit boundary and one end-to-end through the route. This **supersedes the claim in the #3163 entry above that "a member is readable through a virtual exactly when its own `GET` would succeed"**: that was true of the REST `GET /api/v1/repositories/{key}`, which is also action-blind, but not of the `/v2` read gate, which is not. The shared SQL fragment is deliberately **unchanged** — it is also what repository *listing* evaluates, where "any grant means you may see this repository exists" is the intended contract, and narrowing it would have silently changed enumeration. Public members short-circuit ahead of the action check so the anonymous read baseline (#2329) is preserved, admins short-circuit, and a failed lookup drops the member rather than widening the set.

  **Am I affected?** Only if you have granted a principal a fine-grained rule on a private repository whose `actions` array omits `read` (a publish-only CI identity with `{write}`, or a cleanup identity with `{delete}`), or a role assignment to a custom role carrying no `read` — **and** that principal pulls that repository's content through a virtual repository rather than directly. That combination now returns not-found where it previously returned bytes. Grant those principals `read` on the member if they are also expected to read through the virtual; a direct pull was already refusing them, so nothing that worked on the direct path changes. Read-granted users, admins, public members, token scoping and every listing surface are unaffected. Two residuals are tracked separately and are **not** closed by this change: the virtual `storage_used_bytes` aggregate still sums over any-grant members, and `user_can_access_repo` remains action-blind on the REST content paths (#3331), which is why the virtual path is now *stricter* than the REST direct read of the same member.

- **Attaching a member to a virtual repository is authorized against repository visibility** (#3177). `authorize_virtual_member_mutation` checked the member being attached with a two-line wrapper over the same token-scope predicate, and `create_repository`'s member loop had no member check at all — so a principal could compose a virtual repository out of repositories it cannot read.
- **The PyPI download path is keyed on the normalized project name** (#3183). PyPI carries two name coercions that disagree: `normalize_pep503` drops any character outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` without marking a separator, while `PypiHandler::normalize_name` maps every non-alphanumeric to `-`. `serve_file` keyed the PEP 708 dependency-confusion isolation and the per-member curation gate on the first, then passed the **raw** path segment to the upstream fetch and the proxy-cache key. So `GET /pypi/{key}/simple/acme%20sdk/acme_sdk-9.9.9-py3-none-any.whl` evaluated the gate as `acmesdk` — owned by nobody, blocked by nobody — and then resolved `acme-sdk`, the private package the gate exists to protect, serving and caching the attacker's wheel bytes to an anonymous caller. The same divergence made a curation block on a direct Remote repository evadable by spelling. `serve_file` now takes the normalized name and the raw segment is removed from its scope entirely, so it cannot be reintroduced. Reachable in 1.7.1.
- **PyPI project names are validated against PEP 508 on every route that takes one** (#3186, #3198). The recurring normalizer divergence above has been fixed four times by making two coercions agree; this closes it structurally instead. PEP 503's normalization rule — `re.sub(r"[-_.]+", "-", name).lower()` — *presupposes* a valid name, and PEP 508 supplies the precondition (`^([A-Z0-9]|[A-Z0-9][A-Z0-9._-]*[A-Z0-9])$`, case-insensitive). A name like `acme sdk` is therefore outside the rule's input domain and has no correct normalization; two coercions disagreeing about it is the expected outcome, not an implementation slip. A `:project` segment that fails the pattern is now rejected with `404` — matching pypi.org, which 404s `/simple/acme%20sdk/`, `/simple/_leading/` and `/simple/trailing-/` while 301-redirecting names that merely need normalizing — before any gate, database lookup or upstream fetch, on the simple index, the distribution download and the PEP 658 `.metadata` resource. The twine upload route (`POST /pypi/{key}/`) takes its project name from a multipart form field rather than a path segment, so it never reached that validation and ran `normalize_name`, which *coerces*: `acme!sdk`, `acme@sdk`, `acme/sdk`, `acme sdk` and `acme\u{212A}sdk` were all accepted and published into the existing `acme-sdk` project's namespace, `acmé-sdk` landed in a third distinct project, `_leading`/`trailing-` were silently renamed, and `---` or an empty name stored an artifact at a path reachable at no URL while still charging quota. Uploads now validate with the same constructor the read paths use and return `400` on a name that is not a project name.
- **PyPI uploads must carry metadata that agrees with their declared identity** (#3107). `PypiHandler::validate()` held the name/version-vs-metadata consistency check and had no call site, so a wheel or sdist whose embedded `METADATA`/`PKG-INFO` declared a *different* project name or version than the twine `name`/`version` form fields was published unchecked into the declared project's namespace — an archive whose metadata says `evilpkg` accepted and served from `/simple/realclaim/`. The check now runs on the upload path, reading only the metadata entry out of the spooled body through new streaming extractors (ZIP central directory, or a single gzipped-tar entry) under a byte cap, so a 10 GiB upload is not buffered and a hostile archive cannot turn the check into an unbounded read; ZIP and tar parsing run on the blocking pool. Version comparison accepts PEP 440 equivalence, so declaring `1.0` against a `1.0.0` metadata version is not a false mismatch, and a body that does not parse as the declared archive type is passed through — it carries no metadata to contradict. Only a distribution that *does* carry metadata and disagrees with it is rejected, with `400`.
- **Hosted npm publishes reject non-ASCII package names** (#3007). The hosted publish validator checked length and path shape but not character set, so a Unicode homoglyph name such as `nｕmpy` could reach publication while the proxy path already applied a stricter validator — an asymmetry at the primary write boundary. The check runs after URL decoding and before `publish_package`, so a rejected name never reaches repository lookup, payload parsing, metadata persistence or object storage, and it covers the scoped and unscoped routes including percent-encoded input. It is an explicit ASCII test rather than a swap to `is_valid_npm_name`, which also rejects uppercase names that hosted publishing accepts today; that grammar migration is a separate compatibility decision.
- **SSO logins with an empty subject are rejected instead of collapsing onto one account** (#3204). Nothing validated that a SAML assertion's `NameID` was non-empty, and the NameID is the account key — it is what `get_or_create_user` stores in `users.external_id` and resolves accounts by. An empty NameID is therefore not "a user with no identifier", it is the *same lookup key for every such assertion*: the first empty-NameID login provisioned a row with `external_id = ''` and every later one — anyone else at the same IdP — silently resolved to that account. All four XML shapes reached the lookup as `""`: element absent, `<NameID></NameID>`, `<NameID/>`, and whitespace-only. Extraction now rejects an empty or whitespace-only NameID at the parse boundary before any identity is derived, and rejects `transient`-format NameIDs, which SAML 2.0 Core §8.3.8 says a relying party "SHOULD treat as an opaque and temporary value" and which therefore cannot key a durable account row; absent and `unspecified` formats stay accepted, since §8.3.1 leaves their interpretation to implementations and Artifact Keeper's own `NameIDPolicy` requests `unspecified`. `get_or_create_user` also refuses an empty account key outright for **both** SAML and OIDC, closing the equivalent hole on the OIDC side — OIDC Core §2 requires a non-empty `sub`, but an empty one deserialized fine and passed through both the ID-token and userinfo extraction paths.
- **The inline scan-and-block download gate reaches the virtual-member route of every format** (#3220). `enforce_download_gate` is the shared download choke point for quarantine *and* the repository's scan policy (`block_unscanned` / `block_on_fail` / `max_severity`). Every format's direct hosted route reached it; the virtual-member route of ~24 formats did not, because every virtual-member local serve funnels through `proxy_helpers::local_lookup_artifact`, whose only gate was the raw quarantine predicate. A scan-policy block on a hosted repository was therefore bypassable by pulling the same artifact through any virtual repository that listed it as a member — reproduced in one fixture, same repository and same policy, `403` on the direct route and `200` with the bytes on the virtual one. The gate is applied at the shared helper rather than per format, because hand-repeating the check is what produced the gap. Two consequences had to be resolved for that to be safe: virtual resolution treats an `Err` from a member fetch as "this member does not have it, try the next one", so a policy block had to be made **terminal** rather than a silent fallback to another member or upstream; and the quarantine half stays in-process on the row the lookup already selected, so no extra query is added. Two hand-rolled member loops that bypass the resolvers (`helm::download_chart_via_index`, `pypi::serve_file`) are fixed too, as is `maven_local_fetch_storage_fallback`, where a policy-blocked jar still had its whole row-less companion set (`.pom`, `-sources.jar`, `.sha1`, …) served on the direct hosted route as well as through a virtual, because the anchor's verdict is the only verdict those companions have.
- **The download gate is applied on the Generic and Terraform byte paths** (#3143). Two byte-serving paths called the raw quarantine predicate instead of the shared gate, so an artifact every other format would refuse was downloadable through them: `GET /general/{key}/{path}` (and the same handler re-exported at `/api/v1/repositories/{key}/download/*path`, including its `?version=` revision arm) and the Terraform module-archive and provider-binary routes. Generic is gated at the handler, *before* the branch into the presigned-redirect sub-path — a presigned-S3 deployment would otherwise have answered with a `302` straight to the object with no policy check at all. Terraform routes through a new streaming counterpart of the helper the buffered family already used. The gate is deliberately not pushed down into `local_lookup_artifact`'s callers for these paths, because a rejection raised inside a virtual member's fetch closure would be swallowed as a member miss. One scope correction to the issue as filed: the Helm hosted download was *already* gated, one level down through `proxy_helpers::serve_local_artifact`; it gains a regression test pinning that indirect coverage rather than a behaviour change.
- **`block_on_fail` reads the latest scan row per scanner instead of one global newest row** (#3142). This is the residual half of the multi-scanner fail-open closed for `max_severity` in 1.7.1. With more than one engine enabled: scanner A crashes and records `status='failed'`, scanner B then completes clean, the single newest row across all `scan_type`s reads `completed`, and `block_on_fail` never fires — while `classify_scan_state` is any-completed-wins, so `block_unscanned` stays quiet too. An artifact with a crashed scanner was served as fully vetted with `block_on_fail` explicitly enabled. The gate now reads the latest row per `scan_type`, mirroring the `DISTINCT ON` window `recalculate_score` already used, so a clean scan by a different engine cannot mask engine A's crash while a genuine rescan by engine A itself still clears it. The chosen semantics are stated explicitly — **block if any scanner's most recent attempt failed** — because "the most recent attempt" is ambiguous once several engines write independently. `not_applicable` rows are excluded through the existing predicate (covering both the dedicated status and the legacy `failed` + "does not apply" marker); without that, widening from one row to "any scanner" would have started blocking every artifact in every repository where an enabled engine simply does not apply to the format. The old test asserted the bug (`assert!(!post_scan_gates(Some("completed"), true, false).block_on_fail_applies)`) and passed with and without it; it has been replaced.
- **Proxy-cache readers that bypass the metadata sidecar are gated on it** (#3147). The proxy cache's safety model assumes readers consult the `__cache_meta__.json` sidecar — that assumption is what lets a rejected write protect itself by not writing one. Three readers reached the `__content__` key without it. Stale-if-error opened the body first and loaded the sidecar afterwards best-effort, with `unwrap_or(None)` collapsing "absent" and "unreadable" into "serve it anyway with guessed headers"; it served unvalidated bytes with no sidecar vouching for them, served bytes sitting under a negative-cache marker, and handed out entries under an active package age hold. `is_cache_fresh` reported an object fresh when its length disagreed with its sidecar, and the caller turns `true` into a presigned `302` that nothing downstream re-verifies. The write side was closed separately (below), but objects still reach the live key by other routes — the >5 GiB restream carve-out, a crash between the body write and the sidecar write, and any out-of-band write to the bucket — so the read side has to hold independently.
- **Proxy-cache streamed writes are published through a private staging key** (#3152). `CachePersister::tee_stream` wrote a streamed upstream body directly to the live cache key and classified it afterwards; on reject it issued a best-effort `delete` with no version pinning and failures swallowed at `debug!`. Under concurrent cold-cache requests for the same hot artifact a rejected writer's bytes land on the live key, and if the cleanup delete then fails silently a truncated or zero-byte object becomes the permanent current version — observed twice in production. The streaming cache-hit path does no integrity verification against the sidecar, so an entry corrupted this way never self-heals. Streamed writes now target `proxy-cache-staging/<uuid>` under a dedicated prefix and the live key is only ever touched by `StorageBackend::copy` once the write classifies as a commit and any digest validates; a rejected or failed write discards the staging object and never touches the live key. The dedicated prefix (rather than a suffix on the content key) keeps staging objects out of every `proxy-cache/`-scoped listing, makes them independently lifecycle-sweepable, and keeps the content-key length budget from having to grow. `FilesystemBackend::copy` was a bare `fs::copy` onto the destination — non-atomic, and now on the hot path — and is rewritten to copy-to-temp, fsync and rename.
- **Digests already on hand are enforced against cached proxy bytes, and the streamed cache write is bounded** (#2929, #2928). The Remote cargo `.crate` download called the wrapper that hardcodes `expected_checksum: None`, while the *same handler* serves the sparse-index entry carrying the `cksum` cargo itself verifies against — so the digest was decorative. It now resolves the index `cksum` for `{name}/{version}` and routes the download through the digest-gated sibling, which on a split-host registry catches a misbehaving or compromised download host while the index is intact, and catches ordinary corruption or a clean-EOF truncation on either. A body that fails the gate is still streamed to the client, which verifies independently; it is only kept out of the cache. The NuGet missing-file repair refetched from upstream and wrote the bytes back under the artifact row's own storage key without ever comparing them to that row's `checksum_sha256` — a column the `SELECT` already fetched and nothing read — so an admin releasing an artifact from quarantine was approving a hash never enforced against what clients then received; the repair now verifies before the write-back and fails on a mismatch rather than persisting and serving it. A digest is enforced only when it is a bare lowercase 64-hex SHA-256; a `sha256:`-prefixed or otherwise non-canonical value reads as "no digest available", because a value the comparison can never match would reject every entry, cache nothing, and re-pull upstream on every request. Separately, `exceeds_single_object_quota` read `quota_bytes` off the `Repository` that every handler-driven proxy fetch synthesizes, which hardcodes `None` — so the guard was structurally unreachable on every path that serves proxy traffic and a configured quota did not bound a streamed cache write.
- **The bundled scanner CLIs no longer link a vulnerable go-git** (#3307). **The vulnerable dependency is in the third-party scanner binaries Artifact Keeper ships, not in Artifact Keeper's own code** — `github.com/go-git/go-git/v5` v5.19.1 (GHSA-hc8v-wwc9-vgxm, CVE-2026-71556, HIGH) is linked into `grype` in the backend image and into `trivy` inside the scanner-adapter image. Nothing regressed to cause it: neither image changed, and the advisory was published against a dependency both already shipped — a clean scan is a statement about a moment, not a property of an image. The operator-visible effect was on the pipeline rather than on a running instance: `Docker Publish` on `main` began failing `Security Scan` on both Trivy gates, which hard-gate every multi-arch manifest, and would have stalled this release's cut the way #3039 stalled 1.7.0-rc.1. grype moves v0.116.0 to **v0.117.0**, the first release pinning go-git v5.19.2 — v0.116.1 is **not** the fix and still carries v5.19.1, measured against the gate's exact configuration rather than inferred from the version number. No fixed upstream trivy release exists at all, so the scanner-adapter re-pins to the in-house `artifact-keeper/trivy` 0.73.0-r2, which adds a go-git dependency override on top of the existing `oras-go` one; the shipped binary's own Go build info records `v5.19.2`, so the override is proven linked rather than asserted. `.trivyignore`'s own rule would have permitted a suppression here, and that is deliberately not what was done — **suppress when there is no fix; override when one exists and only the packaging is missing.** The bump changes nothing the backend parses: the same fixture through v0.116.0 and v0.117.0 yields identical matches with `relatedVulnerabilities` still top-level on the match, the trap #1375 closed. Nine `.trivyignore` tokens are now provably dead — the pinned digest scanned with no ignorefile finds none of them — and are marked **RETIRED** rather than deleted, because the preflight's superset check would fail while 1.5.x and 1.6.x still list them; removal is #3309.

### Added

- **Migration jobs can rename repositories on import** (#3035): `MigrationConfig.repo_mappings` maps a source repository key to the key it lands under (e.g. `network-team-python` -> `network-pypi`); unmapped repositories keep their source name, and a renamed member of a migrated virtual repository keeps its membership. Targets are validated like a hand-created repository key and two sources may not share one destination; either fails the job rather than migrating under the original name. A target that already exists is reused and the source's artifacts are merged into it, logged as a warning naming both repositories.
- **Curation can verify publisher attestations** (#2955). PyPI and npm curation ingestion now runs on demand, so publisher-trust rules can evaluate packages the proxy has seen. PyPI PEP 740 attestations are verified in process with sigstore-rs, and a package is marked verified only after the DSSE signature, Fulcio chain, SCT, Rekor entry and inclusion proof, integrated time, OIDC issuer, certificate identity, subject digest and publisher owner all match; the result is persisted on the curation package. sigstore-rs 0.14 does not verify the Rekor inclusion proof inside `verify_digest`, so the verifier calls the crate's `InclusionProof::verify` primitives directly. **npm attestations are ingested but not verified**, and fail safe rather than reporting trust, because their subjects use SHA-512 — an upstream limitation tracked as sigstore-rs#596.
- **Galaxy v3 import status is served at `/ansible/{repo}/api/v3/imports/collections/{id}/`** (#3282), the route the collection-upload response now points `task` at. See the entry under Fixed for what it reports and why the document is synthetic; the route is repo-scoped and sits behind `repo_visibility_middleware`, so a private repository's import tasks need a credential that can read the repository.
- **Dedicated timeout knobs for the S3 client**: `S3_BULK_TIMEOUT_SECS` (default 1800) and `S3_CONTROL_TIMEOUT_SECS` (default 30), both accepting `0` to disable (#3180).
- **`TRIVY_ADAPTER_REGISTRY_URL`** — a dedicated override for the registry URL handed to the scanner-adapter, so operators can point the adapter path somewhere without re-pointing grype (#3169).
- **Backups can record and tolerate unreadable artifacts**: `BackupManifest` gains `artifacts_unreadable` alongside `artifact_count` so an existing archive can be audited without re-running the backup, and `allow_partial_artifacts` in the backup metadata makes a partial backup an explicit operator choice (#3170).

### Changed

- **`MigrationConfig.dry_run` no longer writes** (#3035): it gated only the artifact upload, so a preview created every destination repository and wrote `artifacts` plus OCI index rows pointing at bytes it never uploaded. A dry run now logs the destination plan (create or reuse, under which key), skips provisioning and virtual member correlation, and enumerates the source without downloading or writing, so it still reports the artifact count and byte total it would transfer.
- **BREAKING API SEMANTICS: `PUT /api/v1/repositories/{key}/members` (virtual repository members) is a full-set replace** (#2795, ratified in #2899): **this changed in 1.7.0, not in this release** — #2795 shipped in v1.7.0 under the one-line entry "Virtual-repo total aggregation is corrected and member edits are allowed", and the ratification (#2899) that put the semantics in the OpenAPI operation description and pinned them with a regression test shipped in v1.7.1. It is restated here at full weight because neither release's notes said what the change does to a client, and **anyone already on 1.7.0 or later is already exposed**. As of #2795 the request body is the *complete* desired member list, not a partial update. Any existing member whose key is absent from the body is **removed**; members present in the body are inserted if missing, or have their priority updated if already present; an empty `members` array removes every member. Previously the endpoint only reordered members that already existed and returned 404 for a member that was not already present, which made it impossible to add or remove a member when editing a virtual repository after creation. **API clients that send a partial member list in order to reorder priorities will now delete the members they omit** and must be updated to always send the complete member list. The web UI (`virtual-members-panel.tsx` move-up/move-down) and the CLI (`members reorder`, documented as a bulk full-set operation) already send the full list and are unaffected. The behavior is stated in the OpenAPI operation description and pinned by a regression test as of v1.7.1.
- **The blob half of the OCI proxy scan gate honours `scan_on_proxy` and the manifest gate's verdict-freshness rule** (#3105, #3259). The blob re-block — which stops a blocked image being reconstructed one layer at a time — ran unconditionally, while the manifest half short-circuits when proxy scanning is off. The two halves therefore disagreed: after an operator set `scan_on_proxy: false` to unblock a previously-flagged image the manifest served again but every blob `GET` stayed `403 DENIED` forever, with no recourse short of deleting rows from `proxy_scan_results` by hand. The flag is now evaluated per **repository that owns the refs**, not per request: `Remote` honours it, `Virtual` ORs its own flag across the member set (so a virtual with proxy scanning off cannot become a bypass around a stricter member), and `Local`/`Staging` always re-block — because `scan_on_proxy` says nothing about hosted content and hosted repositories normally have no `scan_configs` row at all, so an absent row must not read as "disabled". An earlier revision that applied the flag as one whole-request boolean would have switched the blob re-block **off by default for every hosted OCI repository on the instance**, which is the only blob-level scan enforcement those repositories have. Separately, the blob half had no verdict-freshness rule at all — a bare `EXISTS` on `verdict = 'vulnerable'` with no TTL and no scanner-version predicate — so a verdict the manifest gate would have discarded and re-scanned still blocked that image's layers indefinitely, while on a fail-open repository the manifest itself served. Both blob query sites now pass the verdict's provenance to the manifest path's own predicate, so the two halves cannot drift. A fresh verdict still blocks, and a stale one does not simply serve: hosted repositories keep blocking unconditionally (nothing will ever re-scan them through the proxy-only manifest gate, so a TTL release there would be permanent rather than a deferral), fail-closed proxy repositories keep blocking (their manifest gate re-scans inline before serving a byte), and only a fail-open proxy repository's stale verdicts are released — into exactly the state its own manifest gate is already in. Any `scan_configs` read error keeps the block.
- **`rollup_scan_status` treats `not_applicable` as neutral** (#3144). The rollup matched only `running`/`pending`/`completed`/`failed`, so `not_applicable` fell into the unknown bucket that blocks both terminal collapses — and the default shape for most formats, a completed grype scan plus an OpenSCAP result that declined, rendered as **`partial`** in the Docker tag listing. `completed` + `not_applicable` now collapses to `completed` and `failed` + `not_applicable` to `failed` (a declined scanner must not soften a hard failure), while a result set that is *only* `not_applicable` reports `not_applicable` rather than `completed`, because when every scanner declined nothing was scanned. Mixed `completed`+`failed` still reports `partial`, `running`/`pending` still win, and unrecognized statuses still block collapse pessimistically. No gate reads this rollup — the download and quarantine gates read `scan_results` directly and already handle `not_applicable` — so this cannot un-block anything.
- **`scan_configs.block_on_policy_violation` is documented as inert** (#3144). The column has model, CRUD and DTO plumbing and **zero enforcement readers**; every gate that blocks today reads the separate `scan_policies` table. The `enforce_download_gate` doc comment claimed `max_severity` *was* `block_on_policy_violation`, which is false. The field documentation on the model, the upsert request and the public OpenAPI response schema now state plainly that the toggle is persisted and consulted by no gate. It is deliberately **not** wired up here: as a precondition it would silently disable scan-policy blocking on every repository that never touched the toggle (the column defaults to `false`), and as an additional blocker it needs a severity criterion that has the same problem. Disposition of both columns is tracked in #3246.
- **Updating a permission that collides now returns `409` instead of a `500` leaking raw Postgres error text** (#2826).

### Fixed

- **`ansible-galaxy collection publish` completes instead of crashing** (#3282). #3137 fixed discovery and authentication, which unblocked `install`; `publish` still died with `ERROR! Unexpected Exception, this is probably a bug: 'task'` immediately after a *successful* upload. The Galaxy v3 publish contract is two calls, not one: `GalaxyAPI.publish_collection` (`lib/ansible/galaxy/api.py`) ends with a hard index into the upload response — `return resp['task']` on `stable-2.17` through `stable-2.19`, `return urljoin(self.api_server, resp['task'])` on `stable-2.20` and `devel` — and Artifact Keeper's `202` carried no `task` key. `--no-wait` could not work around it, because `wait` is consulted one frame upstream in `collection/__init__.py::publish_collection`, after the dereference has already raised. The upload response now carries `task`, and the import-status route it points at is served alongside it. **Both halves had to land together**: `wait_import_task` treats a `404` on the task URL as "the import has not started yet" and retries, and the CLI defaults are `--import-timeout` `0` (never time out) with `--no-wait` `default=True` (wait by default), so emitting the field without the route would have traded the crash for an indefinite hang. The emitted value is a root-absolute path ending in `/{task_id}/`, the shape ansible-core documents for v3, which is the one spelling both client generations resolve correctly: `stable-2.17` through `stable-2.19` reduce it to its last non-empty path segment and rebuild the URL from `api_server`, while `stable-2.20` and `devel` `urljoin` it against the origin and poll it verbatim. **The import document is synthetic, and deliberately so**: Artifact Keeper has no asynchronous import pipeline — `upload_collection` validates, stores and records the collection inline — so by the time the `202` is written the import is already finished, and the route reports a completed import derived from the stored artifact rather than progress from a job queue. It reports no intermediate states because there are none. A publish that fails validation is unaffected and never reaches this route: it is returned as a `4xx` from the upload itself, which `_call_galaxy` raises on, so a rejected publish surfaces as a client-visible error rather than a task the client would poll forever. The route is repo-scoped — the lookup is keyed on `(task id, repository id)` — so a task id minted in one repository is a plain `404` in another and is not a cross-repo existence oracle, and it sits behind `repo_visibility_middleware` like every other `/ansible/{repo}/...` route, so a private repository's import tasks stay unreachable without a credential that can read the repository.
- **OpenSCAP `critical` findings are no longer downgraded to `info`** (#3294). `openscap_scanner::convert_findings` classified severities with its own private match — `high | medium | moderate | low`, and `_ => Severity::Info` for everything else. It had no `critical` arm, so content reporting a `critical` rule failure had its **most** severe result filed as its **least** severe one, which is invisible to every gate that reads severity: `Info` has a penalty weight of `0`, appears in no `policy_service` severity array, and has no `info_count` column in any gate's projection. `critical` is not in XCCDF 1.2's `unknown | info | low | medium | high` vocabulary (NISTIR 7275r4 §6.4.4.2), so only non-standard content reaches that arm and the volume is near zero — but when it does fire it is a fail-open, and it now classifies as `Severity::Critical`. Alongside it, `Negligible` — the Debian and Ubuntu security trackers' explicit "triaged, not worth fixing" grade — becomes a **recognised** token instead of a parse miss. On the Grype and native Trivy paths it lands in the bucket it always did (`Info`), so nothing about it changes behaviour; the point is that it now gets there deliberately, and stays there when the ungraded bucket moves. Note that **Harbor-sourced reports are not affected by that arm at all**: `image_scanner::normalize_harbor_severity` rewrites Harbor's `Negligible` to `Low` *before* the token reaches the classifier, and that mapping is unchanged here — deliberately, because `Low` violates a `max_severity = 'low'` policy where `Info` violates nothing, so reconciling the two would have relaxed a gate inside a patch. Artifact Keeper therefore still has two `Negligible` mappings, Grype's at `Info` and Harbor's at `Low`; reconciling them is left to #3243. Both arrive through one shared classifier, `Severity::from_scanner_token`, which replaces the three separate per-adapter fallbacks that let them drift apart in the first place. **No other token's classification changes on any adapter**: Grype and Trivy conversions are unchanged token for token, and on OpenSCAP only `critical` moves. In particular an *ungraded* finding — Trivy's `UNKNOWN`, XCCDF's `unknown`, an empty severity — still classifies at `Info`, which is a fail-open in its own right and a much larger one; closing it moves four gates and can drop a repository's security grade by several bands on the next scan, so it is deliberately not in this patch and is tracked as #3306 for 1.8.0. Refs #3243, which stays open for its later stages.
- **`ansible-galaxy` can reach an Ansible repository again** (#3137). Two independent breaks sat ahead of every collection endpoint, so neither `install` nor `publish` got past API version negotiation. First, the discovery route was registered only as `/ansible/{repo}/api/`; the CLI requests it without the trailing slash, because ansible-core's `g_connect` builds the URL as `_urljoin(n_url, '/api/')` and `_urljoin` strips `/` from every component (`lib/ansible/galaxy/api.py`) — and axum matches `/api` and `/api/` as distinct routes, so negotiation 404'd. Both spellings are now registered, which also fixes the configuration where the server URL is written out as `.../api` and ansible-core skips its fallback probe entirely. Second, the CLI authenticates Galaxy calls with `Authorization: Token <api_key>` (`GalaxyToken.token_type = 'Token'`; only the Keycloak/Automation-Hub variant uses `Bearer`), a scheme the Authorization-header extractor did not recognise, so the value was classified as malformed and every authenticated call — including the discovery probe, which carries the header whenever a token is configured — returned `401`. The `Token` scheme is now accepted as Bearer-equivalent: it resolves to the same internal variant and runs through the identical JWT / API-token validation chain, so it grants no capability that `Bearer` did not already grant, and an unresolvable or empty credential still fails closed with `401`. **`ansible-galaxy collection publish` is not yet fixed by this change** — the upload stores the collection but the response omits the `task` field the Galaxy v3 publish contract requires, so the client raises `KeyError: 'task'`; that needs a new import-status route and is tracked in #3282.
- **The dashboard `total_storage_bytes` now includes OCI blob and proxy-cached bytes** (#3134). `GET /api/v1/admin/stats` derived the figure from a raw aggregate over `artifacts` only, but OCI layer/config blobs live in `oci_blobs` — only manifests land in `artifacts` — so a docker repository contributed a few KiB of manifests while holding GiBs of layers, and legacy backfilled `proxy-cache/%` rows were double-counted against `proxy_storage_bytes`. The total is now summed from `repository_usage_ledger` (`hosted_bytes + proxy_bytes + oci_bytes`), the same trigger-maintained source the per-repository `storage_used_bytes` column and quota admission already read, so the dashboard total equals the sum of the per-repo figures by construction. **API consumers note:** `total_storage_bytes` now includes proxy-cached bytes, so `proxy_storage_bytes` is a *breakdown* of the total, not a disjoint bucket — clients that added the two fields must stop doing so. The daily `storage_metrics` snapshot (`capture_daily_snapshot`) now sums the same ledger, so the storage-trend charts stay consistent with the dashboard total instead of lagging it by OCI blob and proxy-cached bytes. The figures are **logical** bytes — a blob is counted once per repository that holds it, and a blob cross-repo-mounted into N repositories counts N times — chosen so the tile agrees with the per-repository column beside it and with what quota charges; physical, digest-deduplicated bytes remain the storage-GC footprint report's concern. `total_artifacts` still counts `artifacts` rows only, so for OCI it is manifests: blobs are content-addressed objects, not artifacts. Both accounting decisions are now stated on the `SystemStats`, `StorageSnapshot` and `GrowthSummary` schemas (#3249), and the daily snapshot's accounting is pinned by its own regression guard. **Historical `storage_metrics` rows are deliberately not backfilled**: the ledger and its source tables hold no history, so there is nothing from which a past date's OCI or proxy bytes could be recomputed, and any backfill would overwrite real observations with fabricated ones. The one-day upward step a growth series shows where the corrected accounting first lands is a measurement correction, not real growth.
- **Remote and Virtual cargo `.crate` downloads forward the upstream `Content-Encoding`** (#2920). Both download arms hand-roll their response and read `content_length` off the streaming fetch result while dropping `content_encoding`, so a content-coded `.crate` served through an intermediary was written to disk still compressed and failed cargo's checksum with no indication why. The proxy's HTTP client pins `Accept-Encoding: identity` and disables transparent decoding, so the bytes genuinely arrive coded and the header is the only thing that was missing. `Content-Length` and `Content-Encoding` now come from the same source on every serve path, including a cache hit, where the sidecar replays the recorded coding. A response with no upstream coding is unchanged and gains no header.
- **NuGet: the remote-repository cache-repair download now resolves the upstream `PackageBaseAddress` and streams**: when a remote NuGet repository still holds an `artifacts` row for a package whose cached blob has gone missing from storage, `flatcontainer_download` re-pulled the `.nupkg` by appending `v3/flatcontainer/{id}/{version}/{file}` to the configured `upstream_url`, bypassing the service-index discovery every other V3 call site performs (#2917, under the #2775 remote pull-through umbrella). NuGet V3 has no fixed layout — nuget.org serves package content from `https://api.nuget.org/v3-flatcontainer/`, and a configured upstream is normally the `.../v3/index.json` document itself — so that URL 404s and the repair was broken against a real feed regardless of package size. The same re-pull was also buffered under the 8 MiB metadata ceiling (#2181), which does not truncate but returns `502`, so a package legitimately larger than the ceiling (`Microsoft.CodeAnalysis.*`, `Microsoft.ML.*`, `SkiaSharp.NativeAssets.*`, essentially every native-runtime package) failed the repair outright. The repair now takes the same route as the primary cache-miss arm: service-index discovery plus a streamed body teed into the proxy cache, the fix already applied to PyPI wheel recovery in #2192 / #1608 Phase 4c. The proxy-cache key is unchanged, so entries cached by the previous code remain hits, and a blob that is present still streams straight from storage (a warm remote hit no longer buffers the whole `.nupkg` in memory either). Note one deliberate limitation: the repair warms the proxy cache but does **not** re-materialize the blob at the `artifacts` row's own `storage_key`, so the row stays dangling and every later request re-enters the repair branch (served from the proxy cache, with no upstream traffic). Downloads are unaffected, but subsystems that read `storage_key` directly — scanning, replication, promotion, signing, backup — continue to see a missing blob. For a remote whose `upstream_url` is a bare base rather than the service-index document, including an AK-to-AK edge/hub pair, the old concatenation happened to produce the correct URL and did re-materialize the blob, so those deployments lose that incidental healing. Re-materialization is tracked in #2921.
- **npm, PyPI, OCI, generic-repository and cargo sparse-index downloads forward the upstream `Content-Encoding`** (#3149). The same defect class as #2920, in the response builders that #2922 did not reach. `build_tarball_response_stream` (npm) took no `content_encoding` parameter at all, and `oci_v2.rs` and `repositories.rs` contained no reference to the field, so every proxied serve on those paths read the *coded* `Content-Length` off the streaming fetch result and dropped the coding one line later — leaving clients to write bytes they could not inflate. The cargo sparse index had a second cause: it fetched through a helper whose 2-tuple return type structurally discarded the coding, and then pinned the coded bytes into an in-process cache that has no room for one, so every later hit repeated the error. `Content-Length` and `Content-Encoding` now come from the same source on all of these paths; the OCI virtual-blob `HEAD` builds the GET response and drops the body so its headers cannot drift from GET again; and only uncoded sparse-index bodies are memoized. A response with no upstream coding is unchanged and gains no header. Each fixed arm ships its own regression test.
- **goproxy, RubyGems and Hex declare the upstream `Content-Encoding` on verbatim forwards** (#3260). The same class again, in nine sites across three formats that copy upstream bytes through untouched: the goproxy Remote `.info` and `.mod` arms and its Virtual arm, the RubyGems Virtual `gem_info` and `quick_spec` arms, and the Hex Remote `packages`/`names`/`versions` arms plus Virtual `packages`. Nothing on these paths decodes — the shared HTTP client disables gzip, brotli, deflate and zstd and advertises `Accept-Encoding: identity` — so the coded bytes really are what the client receives, labelled as if they were plain. Per RFC 9110 §8.4 `Content-Encoding` describes the coding of the representation as transferred and per §8.6 `Content-Length` describes the coded bytes actually sent, so the pair was only ever correct once the coding was declared alongside it. For goproxy the payloads are `.info`/`.mod` module documents, so a silently compressed file on the `go` client's disk is a correctness problem rather than a header nit, and the Hex Remote arms are the "signed registry bytes pass through as-is" contract, which is the same verbatim property that makes the coding load-bearing. Callers that *parse* the body and serve a rebuilt document — goproxy's `@v/list` filter and its `@latest` JSON path, the existence probe, and the virtual-metadata merge closures — still drop the coding, now explicitly and with a doc comment saying so. The seams were widened in place so every caller breaks at compile time and must state whether it forwards verbatim or parses, and all nine sites share one response builder so their shape cannot drift per format. Two adjacent residuals found while verifying are filed rather than folded in: goproxy's `@latest` and `@v/list` arms mishandle a *coded* upstream body outright (`@latest` 404s because the JSON parse rejects the coded bytes, `@v/list` serves them mangled through `from_utf8_lossy`), which needs decoding of an upstream-controlled body and its own size bounds (#3280); and the virtual metadata resolver still drops the member's `Content-Type`, so Hex serves a signed protobuf as `application/json` (#3281).
- **Maven `download_root` verbatim forwards declare the upstream `Content-Encoding`** (#3211), the same defect in the Remote and Virtual arms of the Maven repository-root path, which forwarded the upstream body untouched while fetching through a primitive that dropped the coding. The primitive is widened in place and every caller classified, so the parse-and-rewrite callers now drop the coding explicitly rather than by omission.
- **The buffered scanned-serve paths forward the upstream `Content-Encoding`** (#3184). #3149 fixed the streaming builders; the two buffered ones were left behind, so on any repository with `scan_on_proxy` enabled the original symptom was still live end to end — `pip install` and `npm install` received a coded body labelled as identity, with `Content-Length` set from the coded byte count. The coding never reached those builders because it was discarded one level up in the primitive's own signature. Widening that primitive in place surfaced a third live instance: the NuGet flat-container buffered arm, which carries no URLs and therefore forwards its body untouched, was dropping the coding for the same reason. The four NuGet arms that rewrite URLs correctly keep dropping it, because the bytes they emit are not the bytes that arrived.
- **PyPI PEP 658 metadata forwards the upstream coding, and a coded wheel no longer reports "metadata not available"** (#3193). `serve_remote_metadata` carried two independent content-coding bugs needing opposite remediations, on the ordinary remote-PyPI metadata path rather than a scan-gated one. The `.metadata` arm forwards the upstream body verbatim while pinning `text/plain; charset=utf-8`, and the coding was not in the fetch helper's return type at all — the third slot of its triple was the `Link` header — so a coded `.metadata` from a gzipping CDN reached `pip` as compressed bytes labelled as plain text. On an upstream `404` the handler falls back to extracting `METADATA` from the wheel, but the extractor opens the bytes as a zip and a coded wheel is not a parseable zip, so a present and perfectly valid wheel answered `404 Metadata not available`; the wheel fetch now goes through the capped variant that reports the coding and the bytes are decoded before the parser sees them, with decode and parse running under one ingest-extraction permit so both halves of the CPU work on upstream-controlled bytes stay admission-controlled together.
- **PEP 658 metadata resolves through a virtual repository's members** (#3175). #3077 fixed metadata routing for remote repositories; virtual repositories still queried metadata against the virtual itself, which owns no artifacts, so every request 404'd. Metadata now resolves through authorized members in priority order, preserving the remote `.metadata` fetch and the wheel-extraction fallback, and applying the same priority-aware dependency-confusion isolation the simple index and artifact downloads use — remote platform-specific wheels are allowed for a locally owned version when they cover a platform not available locally, while remote-only versions and competing builds stay blocked.
- **A `HEAD` is never answered with a method-scoped presigned redirect** (#3181, #3209). A presigned URL is signed for one HTTP method — the method is the first line of the SigV4 canonical request, and GCS's V4 signing is the same — so a URL signed for `GET` cannot validate a `HEAD`. The routers register `get(..)`/`put(..)` only, so axum answers a `HEAD` by running the GET handler, which short-circuited into a `302` pointing at a GET-signed URL; the client re-issued its `HEAD` against that signature and the object store returned `403`. Maven never HEADs an artifact, so this stayed invisible — Gradle HEADs before every download, so **every Gradle build against a repository with `S3_REDIRECT_DOWNLOADS` enabled failed**. The audit of the single 302 builder found three more reachable sites beyond the Maven one: the generic download handler (also re-exported at `/general/{key}/*path`), the shared local-serve helper behind PyPI `serve_file`, and the virtual-repo member cache redirect used by rpm, RubyGems, puppet, hex, huggingface, cran and ansible. Each now declines to presign on a `HEAD` and falls through to the normal serve path, which answers with the artifact's real `Content-Type` and `Content-Length`; a `HEAD` response body is dropped without being polled, so this costs a metadata round trip rather than a body transfer. One dormant helper with no production caller is guarded anyway, because it is the documented drop-in for the unguarded primitive and an unguarded landmine is how this class propagates.
- **Digest-addressed proxied OCI manifests stay immutable in the proxy cache** (#3206). Docker Remote repositories did not behave as pull-through caches: layers pulled through the proxy were re-fetched from upstream on every later pull and the storage volume never grew past one TTL window. Before #2312 every handler-driven proxy fetch synthesized its temporary repository with `format: Generic`, so the cache classifier never reached the OCI rules and content-addressed `v2/<image>/blobs/<digest>` paths fell to the mutable 5-minute default TTL instead of the immutable one; expired entries fail revalidation on the blob path and refill from upstream on every pull. That was fixed for the streaming blob arm in 1.6.0 — reproducing the report against 1.5.1 shows blob sidecars carrying a 300-second expiry and a repeat pull re-downloading every blob, while against current builds a warm pull performs zero upstream fetches and a fully pulled image stays retrievable with the upstream registry down. The same classification gap was still live in the **buffered manifest arm** (the direct Remote manifest `GET`/`HEAD` fallback, also reached by the remote HEAD-blob probe, and the virtual-member manifest fallback), and is fixed here, with pull-through regression guards added so the classification cannot silently regress again.
- **npm packument responses carry `ETag`, `Cache-Control` and answer `304`** (#3052). Every packument was an unconditional `200` with no cache headers and no conditional handling, so `npm`, `yarn` and `pnpm` re-downloaded the whole document on every metadata request even when nothing had changed — and packuments are the largest metadata Artifact Keeper serves, tens of megabytes for popular packages. This is the gap #2780 closed for the PyPI simple index. The tag is computed once from the identity body and stored on both the identity and gzip cache entries, so one packument has one tag regardless of transfer coding; hashing the stored bytes instead would hand gzip and identity clients different tags for the same document and break revalidation whenever a repository's cache eligibility changed. Responses now also carry `Vary: Accept, Accept-Encoding`, required once the response is cacheable at all, since `Accept` selects the full versus abbreviated document and a shared cache must not serve one for the other. The hosted, age-gated and cache-disabled paths get the same headers through their own builder. The conditional check deliberately stays out of the internal packument accessor, because `dist_tags_get` and the cache writer call it and parse the body — a `304` there would resolve dist-tags to `{}` and stop the cache storing anything.
- **Remote npm scoped packuments are cached and catalogued under the decoded `@scope/name`** (#3297). Scoped packages proxied through a Remote npm repository were catalogued — and shown in the web UI's Artifacts tab — as `@scope%2Fname`. The packument fetch sites build the `%2F`-encoded name because private upstreams such as Nexus, Verdaccio and GitHub Packages require the encoded scope separator on the URL, then passed that one string to a proxy helper whose single `path` argument is reused as **both** the upstream fetch path and the local cache key. The encoded form was therefore persisted verbatim as `proxy_cache_artifacts.path` and rendered as-is by `build_catalog_artifact_response`, whose `rsplit('/')` finds no separator and displays the whole encoded string. The npm-protocol responses served to clients were always correct; the defect was confined to the cache key and the catalog row derived from it. All five packument sites now use the fetch/cache-key split primitive the PyPI handler already uses, so the encoded name stays on the wire and the canonical decoded name is the key. **Five, not the four the report identified** — the fifth is the age gate's `npm_publish_time_for_version` probe, which left unfixed would have kept a second copy of every scoped packument under the encoded key and doubled upstream fetches on the age-gated download path. This is not a gate bypass: npm scope policy, on-demand curation entries, quarantine and the age gate all key on the decoded name the router supplies, and the cache TTL classifier deliberately treats `@scope/<pkg>`, `@scope%2f<pkg>` and bare names identically, so the two spellings never diverged in front of a policy check. No migration is needed — entries cached under the old key are packuments, classified mutable with a TTL, so they expire and their stale catalog rows disappear with them.
- **An untouched npm scope-policy form no longer blocks creating a Virtual npm repository** (#3299). Creating an npm Virtual repository — or saving an untouched settings form on an existing one — failed with `npm scope policy is only configurable on remote (proxy) repositories`. The gate is *presence*-based, and every released web UI attaches the scope-policy fields to every npm create whenever that section renders, so an untouched form serialises as `{"npm_allowed_scopes": [], "npm_allowed_name_patterns": [], "npm_allow_unscoped": false}` — where `[]` deserializes to `Some(vec![])` and `false` to `Some(false)`, both of which count as "a policy was supplied". A payload that configures nothing was therefore rejected outright. Fields that are all inactive (empty allow-lists, `allow_unscoped` absent or `false`) are now treated as absent on any target the policy is not configurable on — Virtual, Local, Staging, or a non-npm format — and **nothing is persisted**, so no dead configuration rows are written. An *active* configuration (a scope or pattern entry, or an explicit `allow_unscoped: true`) on such a target is still rejected up front with the same message, preserving the dead-state guard from #2327, and npm Remote repositories are unchanged: empty lists still clear a stored policy on update. The dedicated `PUT /api/v1/repositories/{key}/npm-scope-policy` endpoint is deliberately untouched, so an explicit policy write against a non-Remote target remains an error. The web half of this is fixed on the web repository's main branch but is in no released web version, so the backend fix is what makes every deployed UI work again.
- **`/api/v1/formats` is derived from the compiled-in handler registry** (#3157). `format_handlers` was seeded exactly once, with 13 rows, and every format added since extended only the `repository_format` enum — so the endpoint reported 13 of the 37 handlers the binary actually provides, and `pub`, `composer`, `swift`, `hex`, `sbt`, `cocoapods`, `cran`, `terraform`, `alpine`, `protobuf`, `incus` and the rest were absent. The second half matters as much: the rejection check only rejects an explicit `false`, so a format with no row could never be **disabled** — roughly 24 handlers were silently exempt from the enable/disable surface the other 13 had, and `POST /api/v1/formats/pub/disable` returned `404`. This is not upgrade-only; migrations behave identically on a fresh install. The listing is now derived from the enum and `format_handlers` is treated as an enablement overlay, rather than adding a second seed migration — three separate hand-written format lists existed in the codebase and all three had already diverged, so a fourth would have been a fourth thing to forget. Aliases collapse exactly as handler resolution does (`gradle`→`maven`, every OCI alias→`oci`, `lxc`→`incus`), which is the granularity `format_handlers` is keyed by and the granularity the enablement gate reads.
- **Publishing a Flutter package to a hosted Pub repository works** (#3058). `PubSpec` modelled dependency values as strings, but Pub allows a dependency value to be either a version string or a mapping — for SDK, git, path and hosted dependencies. Every Flutter package declares `flutter: {sdk: flutter}`, so `serde_yaml` rejected the pubspec outright with `dependencies.flutter: invalid type: map, expected a string`, and the extraction helper turned that into the `400` publishers saw on the multipart upload step while step 1 of the flow succeeded. Both dependency maps are now typed as `serde_json::Value` so every dependency form round-trips, mirroring how the cargo handler already solves the identical problem. Remote and virtual Pub repositories are unaffected: proxied metadata is passed through as raw JSON with only `archive_url` rewritten.
- **OCI pushes to classic Helm repositories are rejected** (#3150). An OCI Distribution request against a repository whose format is not backed by the OCI handler now returns `400 UNSUPPORTED` before any write; `helm_oci` and the other OCI-backed formats keep working. Repositories already affected produce a valid `index.yaml` again, because legacy OCI manifest rows are excluded from classic Helm index generation rather than migrated destructively.
- **Docker lifecycle retention counts versions per image, and ages tags by last push** (#2998, #3026). Docker manifest artifacts encode `image:tag` in `artifacts.name`, so the `max_versions` query treated every tag as its own one-item partition and never pruned anything; valid tag-shaped references are now partitioned by image name, preserving registry ports, while `image:sha256:<digest>` references stay distinct full-name partitions so multi-architecture child manifests — which can be stored by digest with no `oci_tags` row — are not grouped together. Separately, `max_age_days` aged a tag by the path-keyed artifact row's original `created_at`, so a moving tag such as `latest` looked 91 days old even though it had just been re-pushed to a fresh digest; live OCI tag-backed manifests are now aged by `oci_tags.updated_at`, which is refreshed on every successful manifest `PUT`, with `artifacts.created_at` retained as the fallback for non-OCI artifacts and unmatched legacy rows, and the same expression used for the preview and the deletion queries.
- **OIDC and SAML users with no directory email address get distinct synthetic addresses** (#3119, #3167). An OIDC provider is not obliged to release an `email` claim, and both claim-extraction paths defaulted a missing one to `""`. `users.email` is `UNIQUE NOT NULL`, so a deployment whose IdP does not release `email` could onboard exactly **one** federated user and then failed everyone else on `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "users_email_key"`. The address is now derived per subject — a sanitized prefix of the subject for operator recognizability plus a SHA-256 fingerprint of the raw subject, so distinct subjects stay distinct despite lossy sanitization, bounded inside `VARCHAR(255)` — under `.invalid`, reserved by RFC 2606, so it can never resolve or collide with a real mailbox. A claim that is present but blank is treated as missing. The SAML path had a narrower relative: it computed its `{username}@unknown` placeholder from the *pre-uniquification* username while inserting the uniquified one, so two distinct NameIDs whose username attribute happens to match got distinct usernames (`alice`, `alice_1`) and the **identical** placeholder address, and the second person could not log in at all. The SAML placeholder is now keyed on the NameID, which is already what the account row is stored and looked up by, so the synthetic address is unique and stable in exactly the cases the account is. Keying it on the username would have been worse than the original bug: the username attribute is self-settable at any IdP that maps it from a user-editable directory field, which would turn an availability defect into a targeted one. Both federated paths now share one derivation, and the addresses shipped for OIDC are unchanged byte for byte. `unknown` is also a resolvable TLD, so the old placeholder could collide with a real mailbox rather than merely with another placeholder.
- **Service-account grants show who they empower** (#2826). Permission list and get responses joined `users` only for `principal_type = 'user'`, so a service account's grant returned `principal_name: null`, and create and update hard-coded both display-name fields to null. The join is now keyed by the `users` primary key for every principal type, matching how permission enforcement itself resolves the row, and create and update return the same hydrated response out of the single statement that writes the row, so the display fields cost no second query and cannot drift from the list and get shape. It is deliberately **not** guarded on `is_service_account`: migration 057 introduced that column in February 2026 but `principal_type = 'service_account'` was not honoured by any resolver until #2499 in July, so in that window the only way to grant a service account fine-grained access was `principal_type = 'user'` plus the account's `users.id`. No migration re-types those rows and the enforcement path still matches them by bare `principal_id` — they are live, enforced grants today, and a type-guarded join rendered exactly those rows nameless, hiding from admins who a still-effective grant empowers. A guardless join keyed on the primary key can never attach the wrong name, only a missing one.
- **Backups never report success when artifact bytes are missing** (#3170, #3171). The backup path collected artifact bytes with `if let Ok(content) = self.storage.get(&key).await`, so a failed read was discarded: not counted, not logged, not in the manifest, not in the job status. The job reported `completed` with `artifact_count=0` and the restore reported `errors=[]` — the worst failure a backup can have, because it is discovered only at restore time when there is no recourse. Every read failure is now logged at `warn` with the key and the underlying error, counted into the manifest's new `artifacts_unreadable` field (`#[serde(default)]`, so existing archives still deserialize), and **fails the job** with a message naming the affected keys unless partial backups were explicitly opted into. The archive and manifest are still written before the job fails — partial data plus a recorded miss count is strictly more useful than discarding both, and the manifest is what lets the restore path refuse the archive later. Restore now reads the manifest and records an error when it declares unreadable artifacts, so a restore can never report `errors=[]` over a known-incomplete archive.
- **`POST /admin/backups/{id}/execute` reports a failed run on the backup, not as a `500`** (#3327). The trigger endpoint called `BackupService::execute`, whose `Err` for a failed run propagated straight through `Result<Json<_>>`, so a run that ended `failed` answered `500 {"code":"INTERNAL_ERROR","message":"Internal server error"}` — and the message #3170 composes for precisely this moment, naming every artifact object whose bytes could not be read, was written to `backups.error_message` and the server log and then discarded at the HTTP boundary. An operator triggering a backup got a bare 500 with no way to learn why. A run that reaches a terminal state has already recorded its own outcome on the backup row; that is a fact about the backup, not about the request, which was understood and carried out. The endpoint now answers with the `BackupResponse` it documents: `200` with `status: "failed"` and `error_message` carrying the cause, or `status: "completed"` for a clean run. **Clients must read `status`, not just the HTTP code** — nothing became quieter, the failure now reaches the caller instead of only the log, and `409` (another backup in progress), `404` and `500` (the run could not be started, or its outcome could not be recorded) are unchanged. `BackupService::execute` keeps its `Err`-on-failed-run shape for the scheduler, whose `backup_schedule_runs` bookkeeping depends on it; the new `execute_run` is what reports an outcome rather than reacting to one.
